        <meta name="robots" content="INDEX,FOLLOW,NOARCHIVE" /><meta name="citation_inbook_title" content="Endotext [Internet]" /><meta name="citation_title" content="The Role of Lipids and Lipoproteins in Atherosclerosis" /><meta name="citation_publisher" content="MDText.com, Inc." /><meta name="citation_date" content="2019/01/03" /><meta name="citation_author" content="MacRae F Linton" /><meta name="citation_author" content="Patricia G Yancey" /><meta name="citation_author" content="Sean S Davies" /><meta name="citation_author" content="W. Gray Jerome" /><meta name="citation_author" content="Edward F Linton" /><meta name="citation_author" content="Wenliang L Song" /><meta name="citation_author" content="Amanda C Doran" /><meta name="citation_author" content="Kasey C Vickers" /><meta name="citation_pmid" content="26844337" /><meta name="citation_fulltext_html_url" content="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK343489/" /><link rel="schema.DC" href="http://purl.org/DC/elements/1.0/" /><meta name="DC.Title" content="The Role of Lipids and Lipoproteins in Atherosclerosis" /><meta name="DC.Type" content="Text" /><meta name="DC.Publisher" content="MDText.com, Inc." /><meta name="DC.Contributor" content="MacRae F Linton" /><meta name="DC.Contributor" content="Patricia G Yancey" /><meta name="DC.Contributor" content="Sean S Davies" /><meta name="DC.Contributor" content="W. Gray Jerome" /><meta name="DC.Contributor" content="Edward F Linton" /><meta name="DC.Contributor" content="Wenliang L Song" /><meta name="DC.Contributor" content="Amanda C Doran" /><meta name="DC.Contributor" content="Kasey C Vickers" /><meta name="DC.Date" content="2019/01/03" /><meta name="DC.Identifier" content="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sites/books/NBK343489/" /><meta name="description" content="Atherosclerosis is the underlying cause of heart attack and stroke. Early observations that cholesterol is a key component of arterial plaques gave rise to the cholesterol hypothesis for the pathogenesis of atherosclerosis. Population studies have demonstrated that elevated levels of LDL cholesterol and apolipoprotein B (apoB) 100, the main structural protein of LDL, are directly associated with risk for atherosclerotic cardiovascular events (ASCVE). Indeed, infiltration and retention of apoB containing lipoproteins in the artery wall is a critical initiating event that sparks an inflammatory response and promotes the development of atherosclerosis. Arterial injury causes endothelial dysfunction promoting modification of apoB containing lipoproteins and infiltration of monocytes into the subendothelial space. Internalization of the apoB containing lipoproteins by macrophages promotes foam cell formation, which is the hallmark of the fatty streak phase of atherosclerosis. Macrophage inflammation results in enhanced oxidative stress and cytokine/chemokine secretion, causing more LDL/remnant oxidation, endothelial cell activation, monocyte recruitment, and foam cell formation. HDL, apoA-I, and endogenous apoE prevent inflammation and oxidative stress and promote cholesterol efflux to reduce lesion formation. Macrophage inflammatory chemoattractants stimulate infiltration and proliferation of smooth muscle cells. Smooth muscle cells produce the extracellular matrix providing a stable fibrous barrier between plaque prothrombotic factors and platelets. Unresolved inflammation results in formation of vulnerable plaques characterized by enhanced macrophage apoptosis and defective efferocytosis of apoptotic cells resulting in necrotic cell death leading to increased smooth muscle cell death, decreased extracellular matrix production, and collagen degradation by macrophage proteases. Rupture of the thinning fibrous cap promotes thrombus formation resulting in clinical ischemic ASCVE. Surprisingly, native LDL is not taken up by macrophages in vitro but has to be modified to promote foam cell formation. Oxidative modification converts LDL into atherogenic particles that initiate inflammatory responses. Uptake and accumulation of oxidatively modified LDL (oxLDL) by macrophages initiates a wide range of bioactivities that may drive development of atherosclerotic lesions. Lowering LDL-cholesterol with statins reduces risk for cardiovascular events, providing ultimate proof of the cholesterol hypothesis. All of the apoB containing lipoproteins are atherogenic, and both triglyceride rich remnant lipoproteins and Lp(a) promote atherothrombosis. Non-HDL cholesterol levels capture all of the apoB containing lipoproteins in one number and are useful in assessing risk in the setting of hypertriglyceridemia. Measures of apoB and LDL-P are superior at predicting risk for ASCVE, when levels of LDL-C and LDL-P are discordant. Here, we also describe the current landscape of HDL metabolism. Epidemiological studies have consistently shown that HDL-C levels are inversely related to ASCVE. We highlight recent clinical trials aimed at raising HDL-C that failed to reduce CVE and the shifting clinical targets of HDL-C, HDL particle numbers, and HDL function (e.g. cholesterol efflux capacity). Furthermore, we describe many beneficial properties of HDL that antagonize atherosclerosis and how HDL dysfunction may promote cardiometabolic disease." /><meta name="og:title" content="The Role of Lipids and Lipoproteins in Atherosclerosis" /><meta name="og:type" content="book" /><meta name="og:description" content="Atherosclerosis is the underlying cause of heart attack and stroke. Early observations that cholesterol is a key component of arterial plaques gave rise to the cholesterol hypothesis for the pathogenesis of atherosclerosis. Population studies have demonstrated that elevated levels of LDL cholesterol and apolipoprotein B (apoB) 100, the main structural protein of LDL, are directly associated with risk for atherosclerotic cardiovascular events (ASCVE). Indeed, infiltration and retention of apoB containing lipoproteins in the artery wall is a critical initiating event that sparks an inflammatory response and promotes the development of atherosclerosis. Arterial injury causes endothelial dysfunction promoting modification of apoB containing lipoproteins and infiltration of monocytes into the subendothelial space. Internalization of the apoB containing lipoproteins by macrophages promotes foam cell formation, which is the hallmark of the fatty streak phase of atherosclerosis. Macrophage inflammation results in enhanced oxidative stress and cytokine/chemokine secretion, causing more LDL/remnant oxidation, endothelial cell activation, monocyte recruitment, and foam cell formation. HDL, apoA-I, and endogenous apoE prevent inflammation and oxidative stress and promote cholesterol efflux to reduce lesion formation. Macrophage inflammatory chemoattractants stimulate infiltration and proliferation of smooth muscle cells. Smooth muscle cells produce the extracellular matrix providing a stable fibrous barrier between plaque prothrombotic factors and platelets. Unresolved inflammation results in formation of vulnerable plaques characterized by enhanced macrophage apoptosis and defective efferocytosis of apoptotic cells resulting in necrotic cell death leading to increased smooth muscle cell death, decreased extracellular matrix production, and collagen degradation by macrophage proteases. Rupture of the thinning fibrous cap promotes thrombus formation resulting in clinical ischemic ASCVE. Surprisingly, native LDL is not taken up by macrophages in vitro but has to be modified to promote foam cell formation. Oxidative modification converts LDL into atherogenic particles that initiate inflammatory responses. Uptake and accumulation of oxidatively modified LDL (oxLDL) by macrophages initiates a wide range of bioactivities that may drive development of atherosclerotic lesions. Lowering LDL-cholesterol with statins reduces risk for cardiovascular events, providing ultimate proof of the cholesterol hypothesis. All of the apoB containing lipoproteins are atherogenic, and both triglyceride rich remnant lipoproteins and Lp(a) promote atherothrombosis. Non-HDL cholesterol levels capture all of the apoB containing lipoproteins in one number and are useful in assessing risk in the setting of hypertriglyceridemia. Measures of apoB and LDL-P are superior at predicting risk for ASCVE, when levels of LDL-C and LDL-P are discordant. Here, we also describe the current landscape of HDL metabolism. Epidemiological studies have consistently shown that HDL-C levels are inversely related to ASCVE. We highlight recent clinical trials aimed at raising HDL-C that failed to reduce CVE and the shifting clinical targets of HDL-C, HDL particle numbers, and HDL function (e.g. cholesterol efflux capacity). Furthermore, we describe many beneficial properties of HDL that antagonize atherosclerosis and how HDL dysfunction may promote cardiometabolic disease." /><meta name="og:url"             <div class="main-content lit-style" itemscope="itemscope" itemtype="http://schema.org/CreativeWork"><div class="meta-content fm-sec"><h1 id="_NBK343489_"><span class="title" itemprop="name">The Role of Lipids and Lipoproteins in Atherosclerosis</span></h1><p class="contrib-group"><span itemprop="author">MacRae F Linton</span>, MD, <span itemprop="author">Patricia G Yancey</span>, PhD, <span itemprop="author">Sean S Davies</span>, PhD, <span itemprop="author">W. Gray Jerome</span>, PhD, <span itemprop="author">Edward F Linton</span>, MD, <span itemprop="author">Wenliang L Song</span>, MD, <span itemprop="author">Amanda C Doran</span>, PhD, MD, and <span itemprop="author">Kasey C Vickers</span>, PhD.</p><a data-jig="ncbitoggler" href="#__NBK343489_ai__" style="border:0;text-decoration:none">Author Information and Affiliations</a><div style="display:none" class="ui-widget" id="__NBK343489_ai__"><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">MacRae F Linton</span>, MD<div class="affiliation small">Stephen and Mary Schillig Professor of Medicine, Professor of Pharmacology, Director, Vanderbilt Lipid Clinic &#x00026; Laboratory, Director, Atherosclerosis Research Unit, Cardiovascular Medicine, Vanderbilt University School of Medicine, 2220 Pierce Ave., 318 Preston Research Building, Nashville, TN 37232-6300<div><span class="email-label">Email: </span><a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="gro.cmuv@notnil.earcam" class="oemail">gro.cmuv@notnil.earcam</a></div></div><div class="small">Corresponding author.</div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Patricia G Yancey</span>, PhD<div class="affiliation small">Research Assistant Professor, Cardiovascular Medicine Division, Department of Medicine<br />Vanderbilt University School of Medicine, 2220 Pierce Ave., 312B Preston Research Building, Nashville, TN 37232-6300</div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Sean S Davies</span>, PhD<div class="affiliation small">Assistant Professor of Pharmacology, Division of Clinical Pharmacology, Vanderbilt Institute of Chemical Biology, Vanderbilt University, 2220 Pierce Ave., Room 506A Robinson Research Building, Nashville, TN 37232-6602</div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">W. Gray Jerome</span>, PhD<div class="affiliation small">Associate Professor Department of Pathology, Microbiology and Immunology, Director, Graduate Program in Cellular and Molecular Pathology, Vanderbilt University School of Medicine, U-2206 MCN, 1161 21st Ave, South Nashville, TN 37232-2561</div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Edward F Linton</span>, MD<div class="affiliation small">Department of Ophthalmology, Vanderbilt University Medical Center, 2311 Pierce Avenue, Nashville, TN 37212</div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Wenliang L Song</span>, MD<div class="affiliation small">Cardiovascular Medicine, Vanderbilt University School of Medicine, 2220 Pierce Ave.,<br />383 Preston Research Building, Nashville, TN 37232-6300</div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Amanda C Doran</span>, PhD, MD<div class="affiliation small">Assistant Professor of Medicine, Cardiovascular Medicine, Vanderbilt University School of Medicine, 2220 Pierce Ave., 383 Preston Research Building, Nashville, TN 37232-6300</div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Kasey C Vickers</span>, PhD<div class="affiliation small">Assistant Professor of Medicine and Molecular Physiology and Biophysics, Vanderbilt University School of Medicine, 2220 Pierce Ave., 312B Preston Research Building, Nashville, TN 37232-6300</div></div></div><p class="small">Last Update: <span itemprop="dateModified">January 3, 2019</span>.</p></div><div class="jig-ncbiinpagenav body-content whole_rhythm" data-jigconfig="allHeadingLevels: ['h2'],smoothScroll: false" itemprop="text"><div id="lipid_athero.ABSTRACT" itemprop="description"><h2 id="_lipid_athero_ABSTRACT_">ABSTRACT</h2><p>Atherosclerosis is the underlying cause of heart attack and stroke. Early observations that cholesterol is a key component of arterial plaques gave rise to the cholesterol hypothesis for the pathogenesis of atherosclerosis. Population studies have demonstrated that elevated levels of LDL cholesterol and apolipoprotein B (apoB) 100, the main structural protein of LDL, are directly associated with risk for atherosclerotic cardiovascular events (ASCVE). Indeed, infiltration and retention of apoB containing lipoproteins in the artery wall is a critical initiating event that sparks an inflammatory response and promotes the development of atherosclerosis. Arterial injury causes endothelial dysfunction promoting modification of apoB containing lipoproteins and infiltration of monocytes into the subendothelial space. Internalization of the apoB containing lipoproteins by macrophages promotes foam cell formation, which is the hallmark of the fatty streak phase of atherosclerosis. Macrophage inflammation results in enhanced oxidative stress and cytokine/chemokine secretion, causing more LDL/remnant oxidation, endothelial cell activation, monocyte recruitment, and foam cell formation. HDL, apoA-I, and endogenous apoE prevent inflammation and oxidative stress and promote cholesterol efflux to reduce lesion formation. Macrophage inflammatory chemoattractants stimulate infiltration and proliferation of smooth muscle cells. Smooth muscle cells produce the extracellular matrix providing a stable fibrous barrier between plaque prothrombotic factors and platelets. Unresolved inflammation results in formation of vulnerable plaques characterized by enhanced macrophage apoptosis and defective efferocytosis of apoptotic cells resulting in necrotic cell death leading to increased smooth muscle cell death, decreased extracellular matrix production, and collagen degradation by macrophage proteases. Rupture of the thinning fibrous cap promotes thrombus formation resulting in clinical ischemic ASCVE. Surprisingly, native LDL is not taken up by macrophages in vitro but has to be modified to promote foam cell formation. Oxidative modification converts LDL into atherogenic particles that initiate inflammatory responses. Uptake and accumulation of oxidatively modified LDL (oxLDL) by macrophages initiates a wide range of bioactivities that may drive development of atherosclerotic lesions. Lowering LDL-cholesterol with statins reduces risk for cardiovascular events, providing ultimate proof of the cholesterol hypothesis. All of the apoB containing lipoproteins are atherogenic, and both triglyceride rich remnant lipoproteins and Lp(a) promote atherothrombosis. Non-HDL cholesterol levels capture all of the apoB containing lipoproteins in one number and are useful in assessing risk in the setting of hypertriglyceridemia. Measures of apoB and LDL-P are superior at predicting risk for ASCVE, when levels of LDL-C and LDL-P are discordant. Here, we also describe the current landscape of HDL metabolism. Epidemiological studies have consistently shown that HDL-C levels are inversely related to ASCVE. We highlight recent clinical trials aimed at raising HDL-C that failed to reduce CVE and the shifting clinical targets of HDL-C, HDL particle numbers, and HDL function (e.g. cholesterol efflux capacity). Furthermore, we describe many beneficial properties of HDL that antagonize atherosclerosis and how HDL dysfunction may promote cardiometabolic disease.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.PATHOPHYSIOLOGY_OF_ATHEROSC"><h2 id="_lipid_athero_PATHOPHYSIOLOGY_OF_ATHEROSC_">PATHOPHYSIOLOGY OF ATHEROSCLEROSIS</h2><div id="lipid_athero.Atherosclerosis_in_Cardiova"><h3>Atherosclerosis in Cardiovascular Disease</h3><p>As the underlying cause of heart attack, stroke, and peripheral vascular disease, atherosclerosis is the major cause of death and morbidity in the United States and the industrial world (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.1">1</a>). The discovery by Virchow more than 100 years ago that atheroma contained a yellow fatty substance, later identified as cholesterol by Windaus, suggested a role for lipids in the pathogenesis of atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.2">2</a>). Indeed, the goal of this chapter is to focus on the role of lipids and lipoproteins in the pathogenesis of atherosclerosis as well as their critical roles in risk assessment and as targets of therapy. The recognition that atherosclerosis is an inflammatory disease has led to tremendous progress in our understanding of the pathogenesis of atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.3">3</a>). First, we provide brief description of the cellular and molecular events in the key stages of atherosclerosis.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Initiation_and_Fatty_Streak"><h3>Initiation and Fatty Streak Phase of Atherosclerotic Lesions</h3><p>The endothelial lining of arteries responds to mechanical and molecular stimuli to regulate tone, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.4">4</a>) hemostasis, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.5">5</a>) and inflammation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.6">6</a>) throughout the circulation. Endothelial cell dysfunction is an initial step in atherosclerotic lesion formation and is more likely to occur at arterial curves and branches that are subjected to low shear stress and disturbed blood flow (atherosclerosis prone areas) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.7">7</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.8">8</a>). These mechanical stimuli activate signaling pathways leading to a dysfunctional endothelium lining that is barrier compromised, prothrombotic, and proinflammatory (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.9">9</a>). In atherosclerosis susceptible regions, the endothelial cells have cuboidal morphology, a thin glycocalyx layer, and a disordered alignment (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.8">8</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.10">10</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.11">11</a>). In addition, these regions have increased endothelial cell senescence and apoptosis as evidenced by ER stress markers (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.12">12</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.14">14</a>). In contrast, less atherosclerosis prone endothelium is exposed to laminar shear stress causing activation of signaling pathways that maintain endothelial cell coaxial alignment, proliferation, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.13">13</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.14">14</a>) glycocalyx layer, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.15">15</a>) and survival (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.12">12</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.16">16</a>). In atherosclerosis resistant regions, the transcription factors, Kruppel-like factors (KLF) 2 and 4, are activated via MEK5/ERK5/MEF2 signaling which enhances expression of endothelial nitric oxide synthase (eNOS) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.17">17</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.19">19</a>). The increased nitric oxide (NO) production promotes endothelial cell migration and survival thereby maintaining an effective barrier (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.20">20</a>). In addition, the expression of superoxide dismutase (SOD) is increased to reduce cellular oxidative stress (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.18">18</a>). In atherosclerosis susceptible regions, reduced expression of eNOS and SOD leads to compromised endothelial barrier integrity (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>), leading to increased accumulation and retention of subendothelial atherogenic apolipoprotein B (apoB)-containing lipoproteins (low-density lipoproteins (LDL)) and remnants of very low-density lipoproteins (VLDL) and chylomicrons) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.21">21</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.22">22</a>). KLF2, KLF4, and NO production inhibit activation of the nuclear factor kappa B (NF&#x02010;&#x003ba;B) pathway. Increased NF&#x02010;&#x003ba;B activation in atherosclerosis susceptible areas leads to endothelial cell activation (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>), as evidenced by increased expression of monocyte adherence proteins (VCAM-1, ICAM-1, and P-selectin) and proinflammatory receptors (toll-like receptor 2, TLR2) and cytokines (MCP-1 and IL-8) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.19">19</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.23">23</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.24">24</a>). In addition, endothelial cell activation leads to increased production of reactive oxygen species (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.25">25</a>) that can cause oxidative modification of apoB-containing lipoproteins (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.26">26</a>). Besides mechanical stimuli, endothelial cell activation is increased by various molecular stimuli, including oxidized LDL, cytokines, advanced glycosylation end products, and pathogen-associated molecules (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.27">27</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.30">30</a>). In contrast, an atheroprotective function of HDL is to prevent endothelial activation and enhance NO production to maintain barrier integrity (see details below) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.31">31</a>).</p><div id="lipid_athero.F1" class="figure bk_fig"><div class="graphic"><img src="/sites/books/NBK343489/bin/lipid_athero-Image001.jpg" alt="Figure 1. . Initiation of the atherosclerotic lesion." /></div><h3><span class="label">Figure 1. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Initiation of the atherosclerotic lesion. The fatty streak phase of atherosclerosis begins with dysfunctional endothelial cells and the retention of apoB-containing lipoproteins (LDL, VLDL, and apoE remnants) in the subendothelial space. Retained lipoproteins are modified (oxidation, glycation, enzymatic), which, along with other atherogenic factors, promotes activation of endothelial cells. Activated endothelial cells have increased expression of monocyte interaction/adhesion molecules (selectins, VCAM-1) and chemoattractants (MCP-1) leading to attachment and transmigration of monocytes into the intimal space. Activated endothelial cells also promote the recruitment of other immune cells including dendritic cells, mast cells, regulatory T (T-reg) cells, and T helper 1 (Th-1) cells. The monocytes differentiate into macrophages and express receptors that mediate the internalization of VLDL, apoE remnants, and modified LDL to become foam cells. In addition, inflammatory signaling pathways are activated in macrophage foam cells leading to more cell recruitment and LDL modification.</p></div></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.Immune_Cell_Recruitment_and"><h3>Immune Cell Recruitment and Foam Cell Formation</h3><p>Activation of endothelial cells causes a monocyte recruitment cascade involving rolling, adhesion, activation and transendothelial migration (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>). Selectins, especially P-selectin, mediate the initial rolling interaction of monocytes with the endothelium (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.32">32</a>). Monocyte adherence is then promoted by endothelial cell immunoglobulin-G proteins including VCAM-1 and ICAM-1 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.32">32</a>). Potent chemoattractant factors such as MCP-1 and IL-8 then induce migration of monocytes into the subendothelial space (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.33">33</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.35">35</a>). Ly6<sup>hi</sup> monocytes, versus Ly6<sup>lo</sup>, preferentially migrate into the subendothelial space to convert to proinflammatory macrophages in mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.36">36</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.38">38</a>). The enhanced migration of Ly6<sup>hi</sup> versus Ly6<sup>lo</sup> monocytes likely results from increased expression of functional P-selectin glycoprotein ligand-1 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.39">39</a>). In addition, the number of blood monocytes originating from the bone marrow and spleen, especially Ly6<sup>hi</sup> cells, increases in response to hypercholesterolemia (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.36">36</a>). Furthermore, hypercholesterolemia and atherosclerosis increase monocytosis in humans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.40">40</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.41">41</a>). Importantly, increased numbers of inflammatory CD14<sup>++</sup>CD16<sup>+</sup> monocytes independently predicted cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, and stroke in patients undergoing elective coronary angiography (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.42">42</a>). Intimal macrophages also result from proliferation of monocyte/macrophages, especially in more advanced lesions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.43">43</a>). During the initial fatty streak phase of atherosclerosis (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>), the monocyte-derived macrophages internalize the retained apoB-containing lipoproteins, which are degraded in lysosomes, where excess free cholesterol is trafficked to the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) to be esterified by acyl CoA:cholesterol acyltransferase (ACAT), and the resulting cholesteryl ester (CE) is packaged into cytoplasmic lipid droplets, which are characteristic of foam cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.42">42</a>) (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.44">44</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.45">45</a>). Modification of apoB lipoproteins via oxidation and glycation enhances their uptake through a number of receptors not down-regulated by cholesterol including CD36, scavenger receptor A, and lectin-like receptor family (see details below) (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.46">46</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.47">47</a>). Enzyme-mediated aggregation of apoB lipoproteins enhances uptake via phagocytosis (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.48">48</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.49">49</a>). In addition, native remnant lipoproteins can induce foam cell formation via a number of apoE receptors (LRP1 and VLDLR) (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.50">50</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.51">51</a>). Uptake of native LDL by fluid phase pinocytosis may also contribute to foam cell formation (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.52">52</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.53">53</a>).</p><div id="lipid_athero.F2" class="figure bk_fig"><div class="graphic"><img src="/sites/books/NBK343489/bin/lipid_athero-Image002.jpg" alt="Figure 2. . Macrophage Cholesterol Metabolism." /></div><h3><span class="label">Figure 2. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Macrophage Cholesterol Metabolism. Native LDL is recognized by the LDL receptor (LDLR). The LDL is endocytosed and trafficked to lysosomes, where the cholesteryl ester (CE) is hydrolyzed to free cholesterol (FC) by the acid lipase. The FC is transported to the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) to be esterified by acyl CoA:cholesterol acyltransferase (ACAT). Increased FC in an ER regulatory pool initiates a signaling cascade resulting in down-regulation of the LDL receptor. Cholesterol regulation of the LDLR prevents foam cell formation via this receptor in the setting of hypercholesterolemia. ApoB containing lipoproteins that also contain apoE (apoE remnants, VLDL) can cause cholesterol accumulation via interaction of apoE with apoE receptors including the LRP1 and the VLDL receptor, which are not regulated by cellular cholesterol. Uptake of native LDL by fluid phase pinocytosis may also contribute to foam cell formation. Modifications of apoB containing lipoproteins induce significant cholesterol accumulation via a number of mechanisms. Enzyme-mediated aggregation of apoB lipoproteins enhances uptake via phagocytosis. Oxidation and/or glycation enhances internalization via a number of receptors that are not regulated by cholesterol, including CD36, scavenger receptor A (SRA), lectin-like receptors (LOX), and toll-like receptors (TLR4). The CE generated by ACAT is stored in cytoplasmic lipid droplets, where there is a continual cycle of hydrolysis to FC by neutral cholesterol esterase and re-esterification by ACAT. Cytoplasmic CE is cleared by two main pathways. In one pathway, removal of FC from the plasma membrane stimulates transport of FC that has been generated by neutral cholesterol esterase away from ACAT to the plasma membrane. Alternatively, cytoplasmic CE is packaged into autophagosomes, which are transported to fuse with lysosomes, where the CE is hydrolyzed by acid lipase and the resulting FC is then transported to the plasma membrane. The efflux of FC to lipid-poor apolipoproteins or HDL occurs by a number of mechanisms to reduce foam cell formation. Exogenous lipid-free apoA-I or endogenous apoE that is produced by the macrophages interacts with ABCA1 to stimulate the efflux of phospholipid and FC to form nascent HDL particles (e.g. apoA-I or apoE containing phospholipid discs). ApoE produces the most buoyant, FC-enriched particles. ABCA1 plays a major role in the clearance of cytoplasmic CE via autophagy. The apoA-I/apoE discs as well as mature HDL containing apoA-I and/or ApoE stimulate FC efflux via three major mechanisms including ABCG1, SR-BI, and aqueous diffusion. ABCG1 may also play a role in the intracellular trafficking of cholesterol.</p></div></div><p>The triggering of macrophage inflammatory pathways is also a critical event in lesion development. Inflammatory M1 phenotype macrophages exhibit increased oxidative stress, impaired cholesterol efflux and enhanced cytokine/chemokine secretion, leading to more LDL/remnant oxidation, endothelial cell activation, monocyte recruitment, and foam cell formation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.54">54</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.59">59</a>). Oxidative stress, modified lipoproteins, and other lesion factors (bioactive lipids, pattern recognition molecules, cytokines) are capable of inducing inflammation via receptors (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.54">54</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.55">55</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.60">60</a>). In addition, plasma membrane cholesterol in macrophage foam cells enhances signaling via inflammatory receptors (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.61">61</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.62">62</a>). Recently, inflammasome activation of IL-1&#x003b2; and IL-18 has been implicated in atherogenesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.63">63</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.64">64</a>). Indeed, a recent clinical trial showed that subjects treated with the IL-1&#x003b2; monoclonal antibody, canakinumab, had a significantly lower rate of recurrent cardiovascular events which were independent of cholesterol lowering (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.65">65</a>). Macrophage foam cell formation and cholesterol dependent inflammatory receptor signaling can be reduced by the removal of cholesterol by atheroprotective HDL and apoA-I via a number of mechanisms including ABCA1, ABCG1, SR-BI, and aqueous diffusion (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.61">61</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.66">66</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.68">68</a>) (see details below). Lipid-poor apoA-I stimulates efflux via ABCA1, whereas lipidated apoA-I or mature HDL are the main drivers of efflux via ABCA1, ABCG1, SR-BI, and aqueous diffusion (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.61">61</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.69">69</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.71">71</a>). Cytoplasmic CE is cleared by two major pathways. One route involves the hydrolysis of cytoplasmic CE by neutral cholesterol esterase and the resulting free cholesterol is mobilized away from the ACAT pool (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.72">72</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.73">73</a>) and made available for efflux via ABCA1, ABCG1, SR-BI, and aqueous diffusion (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>). Alternatively, cytoplasmic CE is packaged into autophagosomes, which are trafficked to lysosomes, where the CE is hydrolyzed by acid lipase(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.73">73</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.74">74</a>), generating free cholesterol that is made available for efflux mainly via ABCA1(<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.73">73</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.74">74</a>). Furthermore, HDL and apoA-I protect against atherosclerosis by reducing inflammation via mechanisms independent of cholesterol efflux (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.31">31</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.75">75</a>) (see details below). In addition, small non-coding RNAs have been found to impact atherosclerosis development by regulating inflammation and/or cholesterol homeostasis in different cell types in lesions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.76">76</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.77">77</a>). MiR-33a and MiR-33b promote atherosclerosis by impairing cholesterol efflux and promoting inflammatory M1 macrophage conversion (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.78">78</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.80">80</a>).Other microRNAs including MiR-223 and MiR-93 exhibit atheroprotective effects by increasing cholesterol efflux and conversion to the anti-inflammatory M2 macrophage phenotype (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.76">76</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.81">81</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.83">83</a>). HDL carry small non-coding RNAs (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.77">77</a>), which can also reduce or promote atherosclerosis development depending upon composition of individual non-coding RNAs (see details below).</p><p>Although macrophages are the main infiltrating cells, other cells contribute to the development of lesions including dendritic cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.84">84</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.85">85</a>), mast cells, T cells, and B cells (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.86">86</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.87">87</a>). Dendritic cells promote the priming of reactive T cell clones and secrete cytokines, functioning in a largely pro-inflammatory capacity(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.88">88</a>). They also take up lipid, which leads to inflammasome activation and increased pro-inflammatory cytokine secretion (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.89">89</a>). Mast cells produce interferon-&#x003b3; (IFN&#x003b3;) and IL-6 and appear to promote lesion development (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.90">90</a>). Atherosclerotic plaques also contain a significant number of adaptive immune cells, including T and B lymphocytes. The role of T cells is subset-dependent and atherosclerotic plaques have been shown to contain CD4<sup>+</sup> and CD8<sup>+</sup> effector T cells as well as T helper 1 (Th-1), Th-2, Th-17, and regulatory T (T-reg) cells. Antigen-specific Th-1 cells produce IFN&#x003b3; that converts macrophages to a proinflammatory M1 phenotype. Th-17 cells have also been identified in atherosclerotic plaques and have been shown to produce IFN&#x003b3;. However, their specific role in atherosclerosis has not yet been elucidated (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.91">91</a>). Classical T-reg cells produce anti-inflammatory cytokines (TGF-&#x003b2; and IL-10) and inhibit activation of Th-1 cells, leading to more anti-inflammatory M2 macrophages. As atherosclerosis progresses, T effector cell numbers increase or remain constant, while T-reg numbers decline. This reduction in T-regs is due in part to their heightened susceptibility to cell death as well as their impaired trafficking into lesions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.91">91</a>). Further, T-regs may appear fewer in number because they undergo phenotypic switching into other T-reg subtypes. Several subclasses of these &#x02018;former&#x02019; T-regs have been identified in the atherosclerotic lesions of mice, including Th1-Tregs (CD4<sup>+</sup>CCR5<sup>+</sup>IFN-&#x003b3;<sup>+</sup>FoxP3<sup>+</sup>T-bet<sup>+</sup>) and T follicular helper cells (CXCR5<sup>+</sup>PD1<sup>+</sup>Bcl6<sup>+</sup>CD62L<sup>lo</sup>CD44<sup>hi</sup>CD4<sup>+</sup>Foxp3<sup>-</sup>), and these have been shown to have both impaired regulatory and enhanced inflammatory function, therefore contributing to atheroprogression (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.91">91</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.92">92</a>). B cells preferentially reside in the adventitial layer of arteries neighboring sites of plaque, in regions known as tertiary lymphoid organs (TLOs). The function of B lymphocytes is also subset dependent, with B-1 cells being atheroprotective and B-2 cells being atherogenic. B-1 cells undergo limited or no affinity maturation and produce natural antibodies (NAbs) that have broad specificity and low binding affinity. Among these are NAbs, found within atherosclerotic plaques, that can bind to oxidation motifs in LDL and block the uptake of oxLDL by macrophages (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.93">93</a>). Mice engineered to overexpress a single-chain variable fragment of E06, an IgM NAb directed against oxidized phospholipids (oxPL), were found to have reduced atherosclerosis and features consistent with greater overall plaque stability, confirming the atheroprotective nature of these B-1 cell-derived antibodies (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.94">94</a>). B-2 cells produce high-affinity IgA, IgE and IgG antibodies. While the role of IgA in atherosclerosis remains controversial, IgG and IgE are atherogenic. IgG forms immune complexes with oxLDL and promotes an inflammatory macrophage phenotype while IgE also stimulates macrophages and mast cells to produce proatherogenic cytokines (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.95">95</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.ApoE_in_Atherosclerosis"><h3>ApoE in Atherosclerosis</h3><p>In addition to apoA-I and HDL, the endogenous production of apoE by macrophages is critical in preventing atherosclerotic lesion formation. The majority of apoE in plasma is produced by the liver, but macrophages are responsible for producing 5 -10% of apoE in plasma (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.96">96</a>). ApoE serves as the ligand for clearance of all of the apoB containing lipoproteins from the blood by the liver except for LDL. Gene knockout of apoE in mice results in hypercholesterolemia and spontaneous atherosclerotic lesion development (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.97">97</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.98">98</a>). Hence, ApoE deficient mice have been used widely to study mechanisms of atherosclerotic lesion development. Bone marrow transplantation studies were used to examine the role of macrophage apoE in lipoprotein metabolism. Transplantation of <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice with wildtype bone marrow, resulted in normalization of plasma cholesterol levels and protection from atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.99">99</a>), demonstrating the ability of macrophage apoE to exchange between lipoproteins and to serve as a vehicle for cellular gene therapy of atherosclerosis. Furthermore, reconstitution of wildtype (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.100">100</a>) or LDL receptor deficient mice(<i>Ldlr</i>
<sup>-/-</sup>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.101">101</a>) with <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> bone marrow accelerates atherosclerotic lesion development without affecting plasma cholesterol levels, demonstrating an atheroprotective role for macrophage apoE. Interestingly, ApoE protects against atherosclerosis via several mechanisms. Expression of apoE by hematopoietic stem cells reduces monocyte proliferation and infiltration into the intima (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.102">102</a>). In addition, apoE on apoB lipoproteins reduces the lysosomal accumulation of cholesterol by enhancing the expression of acid lipase (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.103">103</a>). Importantly, secretion of apoE by macrophages stimulates efflux in the absence and presence of exogenous acceptors, including HDL and lipid-free apoA-I (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.104">104</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.107">107</a>). Recent studies demonstrated that macrophage apoE facilitates reverse cholesterol transport <i>in vivo</i> (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.108">108</a>). Macrophage apoE stimulates phospholipid and cholesterol efflux via ABCA1, and the apoE particles formed then promote cholesterol efflux through ABCG1, SR-BI, and aqueous diffusion (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.104">104</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.109">109</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.111">111</a>). Endogenous apoE is required for efficient formation of the most buoyant, cholesterol-enriched particles by macrophages (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.104">104</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.112">112</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.116">116</a>). In addition to cholesterol efflux, macrophage apoE prevents inflammation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.117">117</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.120">120</a>) and oxidative stress (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.121">121</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.124">124</a>). The local production of apoE is likely a critical atheroprotective mechanism considering that areas of atherosclerotic lesions have limited accessibility to plasma apoA-1 and HDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.100">100</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.101">101</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.125">125</a>). Humans express three common apoE polymorphisms that predict CAD rates independently from plasma cholesterol levels (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.126">126</a>). ApoE3 (C112, R158) is the most common isoform and is functionally similar to mouse apoE. Compared to apoE3 and apoE2 (C112, C158), apoE4 (R112, R158) are impaired in stimulating cholesterol efflux (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.127">127</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.130">130</a>) and in preventing inflammation and oxidation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.117">117</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.124">124</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.131">131</a>). Consistent with the compromised function of apoE4, human carriers exhibit increased risk of CAD compared to humans expressing apoE3 or apoE2 (heterozygous) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.126">126</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.132">132</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.133">133</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Progression_to_Advanced_Ath"><h3>Progression to Advanced Atherosclerotic Lesions</h3><p>Fatty streaks do not result in clinical complications and can even undergo regression. However, once smooth muscle cells infiltrate, and the lesions become more advanced, regression is less likely to occur (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.134">134</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.135">135</a>). Small populations of vascular smooth muscle cells (VSMCs) already present in the intima proliferate in response to growth factors produced by inflammatory macrophages (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.136">136</a>). In addition, macrophage-derived chemoattractants cause tunica media smooth muscle cells to migrate into the intima and proliferate (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF3" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF3">Figure 3</a>). Critical smooth muscle cell chemoattractants and growth factors include PDGF isoforms, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.137">137</a>) matrix metalloproteinases, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.138">138</a>) fibroblast growth factors, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.139">139</a>) and heparin-binding epidermal growth factor (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF3" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF3">Figure 3</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.140">140</a>). HDL prevents smooth muscle cell chemokine production and proliferation. The accumulating VSMCs produce a complex extracellular matrix composed of collagen, proteoglycans, and elastin to form a fibrous cap over a core comprised of foam cells (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F4/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF4" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF4">Figure 4</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.141">141</a>). A vital component of the fibrous cap is collagen, and macrophage-derived TGF-&#x003b2; stimulates its production (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F4/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF4" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF4">Figure 4</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.142">142</a>). In addition, HDL maintains plaque stability by inhibiting degradation of the fibrous cap extracellular matrix through its anti-elastase activity (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.143">143</a>). A subset of VSMCs accumulates CE and resides in the lesion core (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF3" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF3">Figures 3</a> and <a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F4/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF4" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF4">4</a>). This smooth muscle cell phenotype produces less &#x003b1;-actin and expresses macrophage markers, including CD68, F4/80 and Mac2 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.144">144</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.146">146</a>). While studies have shown that VSMCs express the VLDL receptor and various scavenger receptors, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.145">145</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.147">147</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.148">148</a>) data showing that these cells robustly load with CE, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.147">147</a>) similar to macrophages via these mechanisms is lacking. As lesions advance, substantial extracellular lipid accumulates in the core, in part due to large CE-rich particles arising from dead macrophage foam cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.149">149</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.150">150</a>). Earlier <i>in vitro</i> studies showed that these CE-rich particles effectively cholesterol load VSMCs (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.151">151</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.152">152</a>). Regardless of the mechanisms of cholesterol enrichment, VSMCs compared to macrophages are inefficient at lysosomal processing and trafficking of cholesterol (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.152">152</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.153">153</a>) and express much less ABCA1(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.154">154</a>), which all contribute to impaired cholesterol efflux (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.155">155</a>). However, macrophages in more advanced plaques also have reduced lysosome function and trapping of free and esterified cholesterol within their lysosomes contributes to the overall sterol accumulation in the lesion (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.156">156</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.158">158</a>). The reduced lysosome function appears multifactorial but includes direct and indirect inhibition of lysosomal acid lipase, the enzyme responsible for hydrolysis of cholesteryl esters in lysosomes, and a reduced capacity for transferring cholesterol from lysosomes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.159">159</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.162">162</a>). In cell culture models of human macrophage foam cells, the inability to clear cholesterol from macrophages with compromised lysosome function continues even in the presence of compounds that stimulate efflux (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.161">161</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.163">163</a>). Proteomic analysis of foam cells shows that changes in a number of lysosome proteases are related to macrophage sterol accumulation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.164">164</a>). Thus, at least in the advanced stages of atherosclerosis, lysosome dysfunction contributes to the overall lesion severity. As the intimal volume enlarges due to accumulating cells, there is vascular remodeling to lessen protrusion of the lesion into the lumen (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F4/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF4" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF4">Figure 4</a>), thereby decreasing occlusion and the appearance of clinical symptoms for much of the life of the lesion (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.165">165</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.167">167</a>).</p><div id="lipid_athero.F3" class="figure bk_fig"><div class="graphic"><img src="/sites/books/NBK343489/bin/lipid_athero-Image003.jpg" alt="Figure 3. . Progression of the atherosclerotic plaque." /></div><h3><span class="label">Figure 3. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Progression of the atherosclerotic plaque. Macrophage foam cell and endothelial cell inflammatory signaling continues to promote the recruitment of more monocytes and immune cells into the subendothelial space. Transition from a fatty streak to a fibrous fatty lesion occurs with the infiltration and proliferation of tunica media smooth muscle cells. Macrophage foam cells and other inflammatory cells produce a number of chemoattractant and proliferation factors, including transforming growth factor-&#x003b2; (TGF-&#x003b2;), platelet-derived growth factor (PDGF) isoforms, matrix metalloproteinases, fibroblast growth factors (FGF), and heparin-binding epidermal growth factor (HB-EGF). Smooth muscle cells are recruited to the luminal side of the lesion to proliferate and generate an extracellular matrix network to form a barrier between lesional prothrombotic factors and blood platelets and procoagulant factors. A subset of smooth muscle cells express macrophage receptors and internalize lipoproteins to become foam cells. Fibrous fatty lesions are less likely to regress than fatty streaks.</p></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.F4" class="figure bk_fig"><div class="graphic"><img src="/sites/books/NBK343489/bin/lipid_athero-Image004.jpg" alt="Figure 4. . Features of the stable fibrous plaque." /></div><h3><span class="label">Figure 4. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Features of the stable fibrous plaque. As the cell volume of the intima increases, there is vascular remodeling so that the lumen is only partially occluded, substantially lessening clinical events resulting from occlusion. The stable plaque contains a generous fibrous cap composed of layers of smooth muscle cells ensconced in a substantial extracellular matrix network of collagen, proteoglycans, and elastin. The thick fibrous cap of the stable plaque provides an effective barrier preventing plaque rupture and exposure of lesion prothrombotic factors to blood, thereby limiting thrombus formation and clinical events. Maintenance of a thick fibrous cap is enabled by regulation of the inflammatory status of the foam cell core of the lesion. Regulatory T (T-reg) cells produce transforming growth factor-&#x003b2; (TGF-&#x003b2;) and IL-10. In addition, T-reg cells inhibit antigen-specific activation of T helper 1 (Th-1) cell to produce interferon gamma (IFNg). Increased TGF-&#x003b2; and IL-10 and decreased IFNg reduce the proinflammatory macrophage phenotype leading to reduced cell death, effective efferocytosis (phagocytosis of dead cells), and anti-inflammatory cytokine production (i.e. TGF-&#x003b2;, IL-10). Thus, stable plaques have small necrotic cores containing macrophage debris and extracellular lipid resulting from secondary necrosis of noninternalized apoptotic macrophage foam cells. The production of TGF-&#x003b2; by T-reg cells and macrophages maintains fibrous cap quality by being a potent stimulator of collagen production in smooth muscle cells.</p></div></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.Vulnerable_Plaque_Formation"><h3>Vulnerable Plaque Formation and Rupture</h3><p>The advanced atherosclerotic lesion is essentially a nonresolving inflammatory condition leading to formation of the vulnerable plaque, increasing the risk of plaque rupture. The vulnerable plaque is characterized by two fundamental morphological changes: 1) Formation of a necrotic core and 2) Thinning of the fibrous cap. Sections of the atheroma with a deteriorated fibrous cap are subject to rupture (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F4/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF4" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF4">Figures 4</a> and <a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">5</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.168">168</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.169">169</a>). A recent lipidomics study showed that stable versus unstable plaques have different lipid subspecies profiles (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.170">170</a>). Compared to plasma and control arteries, stable plaques have increased CE containing polyunsaturated fatty acids (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.170">170</a>), which have increased susceptibility to oxidation. The CE containing polyunsaturated fatty acids are decreased in unstable plaques compared to stable plaques of the same subjects (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.170">170</a>). In addition, 18:0 containing lysophosphatidylcholine is increased in unstable plaques indicating enhanced oxidation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.170">170</a>). Plaque rupture leads to acute exposure of procoagulant and prothrombotic factors from the necrotic core of the lesion to platelets and procoagulant factors in the lumen, thereby causing thrombus formation (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.168">168</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.169">169</a>). Thrombus formation at sites of plaque rupture accounts for the majority of clinical events with acute occlusive luminal thrombosis causing myocardial infarction, unstable angina, sudden cardiac death, and stroke (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.168">168</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.169">169</a>).</p><div id="lipid_athero.F5" class="figure bk_fig"><div class="graphic"><img src="/sites/books/NBK343489/bin/lipid_athero-Image005.jpg" alt="Figure 5. . Formation of the vulnerable plaque." /></div><h3><span class="label">Figure 5. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Formation of the vulnerable plaque. The vulnerable plaque results from a heightened, unresolved inflammatory status of the lesion foam cell core. Antigen-specific activation of T helper 1 (Th-1) cells produces interferon gamma (IFNg) resulting in a proinflammatory macrophage phenotype. The proinflammatory macrophage foam cells exhibit enhanced inflammatory cytokine secretion and apoptosis susceptibility. There is less secretion of the anti-inflammatory cytokines, TGF-&#x003b2; and IL-10. In addition, proinflammatory macrophages have impaired atheroprotective functions including cholesterol efflux and efferocytosis. The defective efferocytosis of inflammatory apoptotic macrophages results in secondary necrosis leading to an enlarged necrotic core composed of leaked oxidative and inflammatory components. This unresolved inflammation causes thinning of the fibrous cap resulting from increased smooth muscle cell death, enhanced extracellular matrix degradation and decreased extracellular matrix production. Areas of thin fibrous cap are prone to rupture exposing prothrombotic components to platelets and procoagulation factors leading to thrombus formation and clinical events.</p></div></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.Macrophage_Cell_Death_and_E"><h3>Macrophage Cell Death and Efferocytosis Influence Plaque Stability</h3><p>The necrotic core results from a combination of accelerated macrophage death and impaired efferocytosis (receptor-mediated phagocytosis of apoptotic cells) (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.171">171</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.172">172</a>). As apoptotic cells accumulate and fail to be internalized by phagocytes, they undergo secondary necrotic death leading to the leakage of intracellular oxidative and inflammatory components, which then propagate more inflammation, oxidative stress, and death in neighboring cells (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.173">173</a>). Multiple triggers likely occur in lesions to accelerate macrophage death, including oxidative stress, death receptor activation, and nutrient deprivation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.174">174</a>). Prolonged ER stress and activation of the unfolded protein response (UPR) contribute to macrophage apoptosis as substantiated by studies showing that apoptosis and the UPR effector, CHOP, increase with each stage of atherosclerosis in humans, but the largest increase is observed in the vulnerable plaque (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.175">175</a>). In diabetes and obesity, accelerated formation of an enlarged necrotic core is likely instigated by defective macrophage insulin signaling (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.176">176</a>) and saturated fatty acids (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.177">177</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.178">178</a>), which are potent inducers of ER stress. In addition, other triggers act in tandem with ER stress to accelerate apoptosis. In particular, activation of toll-like receptors (TLR) (TLR2 and TLR4) and scavenger receptors (CD36 and SR-A) by oxidized phospholipids induces apoptotic signaling (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.178">178</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.181">181</a>). Death is also accelerated by simultaneous suppression of survival pathways such as pAkt and NF&#x02010;&#x003ba;B via these same receptors. Accelerated apoptotic macrophage death is not sufficient to promote necrosis. Apoptotic cells undergo secondary necrotic death if they are not internalized by phagocyte efferocytosis receptors. Necrotic death leads to the leakage of intracellular oxidative and inflammatory components, which then propagate more inflammation, oxidative stress, and death in neighboring cells (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.173">173</a>). The presence of necrotic tissue together with apoptosis is consistent with defective efferocytosis in human plaques. Studies have shown that the majority of apoptotic cells are free in advanced human lesions, whereas in tonsils apoptotic cells are macrophage-associated (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.182">182</a>). Efferocytosis also becomes defective in advanced atherosclerosis through several different mechanisms. First, accumulating evidence has shown that the expression and function of key efferocytosis receptors, MerTK (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.183">183</a>), LRP1 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.184">184</a>), and SR-BI (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.185">185</a>) are impaired in advanced atherosclerosis. These receptors recognize apoptotic cell ligands such as phosphatidylserine (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.185">185</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.186">186</a>). and efferocytosis efficiency is enhanced by bridging molecules such as apoE and MFG-E8 that interact with efferocytosis receptors to enhance their efficiency and also have reduced expression in advanced lesions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.186">186</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.190">190</a>). Compared to apoE3, apoE4 is defective at facilitating efferocytosis of apoptotic cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.191">191</a>). Efferocytosis via LRP1 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.187">187</a>) and SR-BI (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.185">185</a>) also stimulates signaling pathways leading to pAkt production to promote phagocyte survival. In addition, anti-inflammatory signaling (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.185">185</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.192">192</a>) is activated so that phagocytes secrete TGF-&#x003b2; and IL-10 (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F4/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF4" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF4">Figures 4</a> and <a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">5</a>). In addition, efferocytosis may be limited by competition for apoptotic cell binding. For example, oxPLs bind efferocytosis receptors and effectively compete for apoptotic cell recognition. In addition, lesional autoantibodies to oxPL and oxLDL are able to bind to ligands on the apoptotic cell themselves in order to prevent their binding and ingestion. Finally, apoptotic cells in advanced lesions appear to become poor substrates for efferocytosis. CD47, which typically acts as a &#x0201c;don&#x02019;t eat me&#x0201d; signal expressed by live cells, is upregulated by apoptotic cells within human and murine atherosclerotic plaques, allowing them to evade uptake by phagocytes. When given to atheroprone <i>Apoe<sup>-/-</sup></i> mice, a CD47-blocking antibody enhanced lesional efferocytosis and resulted in smaller necrotic cores (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.193">193</a>). Similarly, mice that express low levels of the &#x0201c;eat me&#x0201d; signal, calreticulin, have increased necrotic cores compared to control mice and apoptotic cells from these mice demonstrate resistance to uptake by phagocytes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.194">194</a>).</p><p>Components of the necrotic core promote thinning of the fibrous cap. Loss of extracellular matrix is in part due to death of fibrous cap smooth muscle cells, resulting from macrophage-derived Fas receptor ligand (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.195">195</a>), inflammatory cytokines (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.196">196</a>), and oxidation products (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.197">197</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.198">198</a>). Smooth muscle cells are inefficient at efferocytosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.199">199</a>) relying on macrophages to internalize apoptotic smooth muscle cells. As such, the impaired efferocytosis by lesional macrophages likely leads to uncontrolled VSMC death (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>). In addition, impaired production of TGF-&#x003b2; by phagocytes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.185">185</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.200">200</a>) reduces collagen production by healthy smooth muscle cells (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F4/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF4" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF4">Figures 4</a> and <a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">5</a>). The extracellular matrix components are degraded by macrophage-derived matrix metalloproteinases, (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.201">201</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.203">203</a>) elastases, and cathepsins (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.204">204</a>). HDL can reduce VSMC apoptosis and elastin degradation induced by elastases (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.143">143</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.205">205</a>).</p><p>Importantly, HDL can prevent efferocyte apoptosis via ER stress by its cholesterol efflux and anti-oxidant functions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.179">179</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.206">206</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.207">207</a>). Furthermore, HDL drives conversion to the anti-inflammatory M2 macrophages which have enhanced efferocytosis ability compared to inflammatory M1 macrophages (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.56">56</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.208">208</a>) leading to increased plaque stability. Once plaque rupture occurs, critical HDL functions may also include prevention of platelet activation and thrombus formation. In addition to the role of HDL in stabilizing plaques, recent studies have focused on the lesional loss of specialized proresolving mediators (SPM) versus proinflammatory factors (i.e. leukotriene B<sub>4</sub>) in promoting uncontrolled inflammation and formation of vulnerable plaques (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.209">209</a>). Studies on human atherosclerotic lesions have shown that unstable versus stable plaques have decreased lipid-derived SPM including resolvin D1 and lipoxin A<sub>4</sub> (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.210">210</a>). In addition, resolving D1 treatment of <i>Ldlr<sup>-/-</sup></i> mice with established atherosclerosis increased lesional efferocytosis and collagen content and reduced the necrotic area and reactive oxygen species content (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.210">210</a>). Similar results were observed in <i>Apoe<sup>-/-</sup></i> mice treated with the phospholipase D derived proresolving lipid, palmitoylethanolamide (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.211">211</a>). Other lipid derived resolving mediators which impact atherosclerotic plaques include maresin 1 and resolvin D2 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.212">212</a>). Protein SPM have also been identified including annexin 1 and IL-10 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.209">209</a>). Administration of lesion targeting nanoparticles containing the bioactive annexin 1 peptide, Ac2-26, to <i>Ldlr<sup>-/-</sup></i> mice with atherosclerosis reduced both lesional oxidative stress and necrosis while increasing collagen content and fibrous cap thickness (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.213">213</a>). Enhancing the lesional IL-10 content also improved atherosclerotic lesion stability (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.214">214</a>). In addition, Treg cells likely control atherosclerotic lesion inflammation resolution as recent studies demonstrated that Treg cells regulate efferocytosis in atherosclerotic lesions by secreting IL-13 to stimulate macrophage production of IL-10 to induce Vav-1 activation of Rac1 and increased efferocytosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.215">215</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Summary"><h3>Summary</h3><p>Atherosclerotic lesions initiate with endothelial cell dysfunction causing modification of apoB containing lipoproteins (LDL, VLDL, remnants) and infiltration of immune cells, particularly monocytes, into the subendothelial space (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>). The macrophages internalize the retained apoB containing lipoproteins to become foam cells forming the fatty streak (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>). Macrophage inflammatory pathways are also activated leading to increased oxidative stress and enhanced cytokine/chemokine secretion, causing more LDL/remnant oxidation, endothelial cell activation, monocyte recruitment, and foam cell formation (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>). HDL, apoA-I, and endogenous apoE reduce lesion formation by preventing endothelial cell activation, inflammation, and oxidative stress and also by promoting cholesterol efflux from foam cells. As the lesion progresses to fibrotic plaques as a result of continued inflammation, macrophage chemoattractants stimulate infiltration and proliferation of smooth muscle cells (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF3" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF3">Figure 3</a>). Smooth muscle cells produce the extracellular matrix providing a stable fibrous barrier between plaque prothrombotic factors and platelets (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F4/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF4" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF4">Figure 4</a>). Unresolved inflammation results in formation of vulnerable plaques, which have large necrotic cores and a thinning fibrous cap (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>). Enhanced macrophage apoptosis and defective efferocytosis of apoptotic cells results in necrotic cell death causing heightened inflammation leading to increased smooth cell death, decreased extracellular matrix production, and collagen degradation by macrophage proteases. An imbalance between inflammatory factors and SPMs is prominent in facilitating formation of the vulnerable plaque. Rupture of the thinning fibrous cap promotes thrombus formation resulting in clinical ischemic cardiovascular events (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F5/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF5" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF5">Figure 5</a>).</p></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.THE_ROLE_OF_CHOLESTEROL_AND"><h2 id="_lipid_athero_THE_ROLE_OF_CHOLESTEROL_AND_">THE ROLE OF CHOLESTEROL AND LIPOPROTEINS IN ATHEROGENESIS</h2><div id="lipid_athero.Metabolism_of_ApoB_Containi"><h3>Metabolism of ApoB Containing Lipoproteins</h3><p>Apolipoprotein B (apoB) occurs in two isoforms, apoB100 and apoB48. ApoB100 is the main structural apolipoprotein of low-density lipoproteins (LDL), and there is only one molecule of apoB100 per LDL particle (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.216">216</a>). ApoB100 is produced mainly by the liver, where it is required for the synthesis and secretion of triglyceride-rich very low-density lipoprotein (VLDL) particles (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F6/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF6" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF6">Figure 6</a>). In the circulation, VLDL is metabolized to the cholesteryl ester-enriched intermediate low-density lipoprotein (IDL) and LDL particles through the progressive hydrolysis of triglycerides by lipoprotein lipase (LPL) and hepatic lipase (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F6/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF6" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF6">Figure 6</a>). In humans, apoB48 is produced exclusively in the intestine through an unique RNA editing mechanism by the apobec-1 enzyme complex (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.217">217</a>). ApoB100 is the full-length protein, which contains 4536 amino acids, whereas apoB48 contains the first 48% of the amino terminal amino acids. ApoB48 is required for the synthesis and secretion of triglyceride-rich chylomicrons, which play a critical role in the intestinal absorption of dietary fats and fat-soluble vitamins. Similar to the metabolism of VLDL, chylomicrons are metabolized in the circulation through the hydrolysis of triglycerides by LPL and hepatic lipase to form cholesteryl ester-enriched chylomicron remnants, which release free fatty acids that can be used for energy by the tissues.</p><div id="lipid_athero.F6" class="figure bk_fig"><div class="graphic"><img src="/sites/books/NBK343489/bin/lipid_athero-Image006.jpg" alt="Figure 6. . Metabolism of ApoB100 containing lipoproteins." /></div><h3><span class="label">Figure 6. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Metabolism of ApoB100 containing lipoproteins. ApoB100 is critical for the production and secretion of very low-density lipoprotein (VLDL) by the liver. Plasma VLDL is metabolized to cholesteryl ester-enriched intermediate low-density lipoprotein (IDL) and LDL particles via hydrolysis of triglycerides by lipoprotein lipase (LPL) and hepatic lipase (HL). In addition, cholesteryl ester transfer protein (CETP) transfers CE from HDL to VLDL in exchange for triglyceride (TG) to HDL. ApoCII and apoCIII are transferred from HDL to VLDL and act as an activator or inhibitor of LPL activity, respectively. ApoB100 is the ligand for hepatic LDL receptor-mediated clearance of LDL. VLDL acquires apoE from HDL, and apoE mediates the clearance of triglyceride-enriched remnants and IDL. In addition, HDL can directly transfer cholesterol to liver via interaction with SR-BI. VLDL and IDL remnants can induce foam cell formation by internalization via apoE receptors on macrophages. LDL, IDL, and VLDL can be modified (oxidation, glycation) and internalized by a number of macrophage receptors including scavenger receptors and lectin-like receptors. HDL and lipid-poor apoA-I reduce foam cell formation by stimulating cholesterol efflux.</p></div></div><p>Static measurements of cholesterol in the LDL pool (LDL-C) represent the steady state of production of VLDL, its metabolism to LDL, and the receptor-mediated clearance of LDL by the LDL receptor (LDLR). Mutations in the <i>Ldlr</i> gene are the most common cause of familial hypercholesterolemia (FH), an autosomal dominant disorder associated with elevated levels of LDL-C and increased risk for premature cardiovascular disease (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.218">218</a>).
