ApoB100 serves as the ligand for receptor-mediated clearance of LDL by the liver (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F6/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF6" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF6">Figure 6</a>). In contrast, apoE mediates the clearance of triglyceride-rich remnants (IDL and chylomicron remnants) either through the LDLR or the remnant receptor pathway (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F6/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF6" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF6">Figure 6</a>). The existence of the remnant receptor pathway was suggested by the fact that patients with homozygous FH, who completely lack LDLR function, have severely elevated levels of LDL-C but normal blood levels of triglycerides. The clearance of these remnant lipoproteins involves binding to heparin sulfate proteoglycans and the LDLR like protein -1 (LRP1) in the hepatic space of Disse, in a process called secretion capture that requires local enrichment by hepatic expression of apoE</p><p>(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.96">96</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.219">219</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.The_Cholesterol_Hypothesis"><h3>The Cholesterol Hypothesis</h3><p>Studies by Anitschkow showing that feeding cholesterol in oil to rabbits caused the formation of atheroma, similar to those seen in humans, demonstrated a causal role of cholesterol in the pathogenesis of atherosclerosis in 1913 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.220">220</a>). In 1939, Muller described families with inherited high cholesterol and increased risk for cardiovascular disease (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.221">221</a>). Yet it would take several decades before compelling evidence from epidemiological studies, such as Framingham (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.221">221</a>) and MRFIT (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.222">222</a>), demonstrated that elevated blood cholesterol levels were associated with increased risk of cardiovascular events (CVE). Subsequently, LDL-C levels were found to be directly associated with CVE (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.223">223</a>); whereas HDL-C levels were shown to be inversely related to risk of CVE (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.224">224</a>). The Seven Countries Studies by Ancel Keys showed that coronary heart disease (CHD) mortality rates were higher in countries with higher blood levels of cholesterol (e.g. Finland, Norway, and the USA) than in countries of southern Europe and Japan with lower blood levels of cholesterol (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.225">225</a>). The high levels of cholesterol were proposed to be associated with the amount of saturated fat in the diet. As such, the cholesterol hypothesis was born, proposing that lowering LDL-C would reduce CVE (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.226">226</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Response_to_Retention_Hypot"><h3>Response to Retention Hypothesis for the Initiation of Atherosclerosis</h3><p>The response to retention hypothesis holds that retention of atherogenic lipoproteins in the artery wall is a critical initiating event that sparks an inflammatory response and promotes the development of atherosclerosis (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF1" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF1">Figure 1</a>). First articulated in 1995 by Williams and Tabas (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.227">227</a>), the hypothesis was based on more than two decades of work demonstrating that apoB-containing lipoproteins are retained in the artery wall by interaction with proteoglycans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.228">228</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.229">229</a>). Proteoglycans consist of a protein core bound covalently to one or more glycosaminoglycans (GAGs). The most common proteoglycans in the artery wall are decorin, biglycan, perlecan, versican, and syndecan-4 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.230">230</a>). There is ionic binding between the positively charged GAGs and negatively charged amino acids of apoB100 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.229">229</a>). Boren <i>et al.</i> identified the principal proteoglycan-binding site in LDL and showed that a single point mutation in apoB100 impaired binding to proteoglycans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.231">231</a>). The major proteoglycan binding site consists of residues 3359-3369 in apoB100 (site B), which is in the C-terminal half of apoB100. Furthermore, mutation of &#x0201c;site B&#x0201d; in mice resulted in reduced retention of apoB100 in the artery wall and reduced atherosclerosis, providing <i>in vivo</i> support for the response to retention hypothesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.232">232</a>). Subsequently, proteoglycan binding sites were identified for apoB48 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.233">233</a>) and a second site (site A) on apoB100, which is exposed when LDL is modified by secreted phospholipase A2 (sPLA2), forming a small dense LDL particle (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.234">234</a>).</p><p>Surprisingly, native LDL, despite the strong evidence for its critical role in promoting atherosclerosis, does not induce macrophage foam cell formation or much in the way of inflammation <i>in vitro</i>. These observations led to the hypothesis that LDL has to be modified to promote foam cell formation and induce inflammation. Binding of proteoglycans induces structural changes in LDL impacting both the configuration of apoB100 and the lipid composition (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.234">234</a>). Hence, the binding of LDL to proteoglycans makes the LDL more susceptible to oxidation and aggregation, which promotes foam cell formation and a proinflammatory response, and the process is self-perpetuating. Oxidized LDL (oxLDL) can induce further production of proteoglycans by vascular smooth muscle cells, retaining more LDL in the arterial wall. Furthermore, macrophages express LPL, which can serve as bridging molecules, binding both lipoproteins and proteoglycans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.235">235</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.236">236</a>). Consistent with an important role for LPL in atherogenesis, the loss of macrophage LPL expression protects mice from atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.237">237</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.238">238</a>). In addition, macrophages secrete sphingomyelinase, which has been reported to act synergistically with LPL to promote binding of LDL and lipoprotein (a) (Lp(a)) to vascular smooth muscle cells (VSMC) and the extracellular matrix promoting their retention in the artery (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.239">239</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.240">240</a>). Furthermore, sphingomyelinase induces aggregation and fusion of LDL particles, promoting increased binding to proteoglycans and induces foam cell formation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.241">241</a>). Thus, interfering with the retention of apoB-containing lipoproteins in the artery wall is a potential strategy for preventing atherosclerosis.</p></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.Oxidation_of_phospholipids"><h2 id="_lipid_athero_Oxidation_of_phospholipids_">Oxidation of phospholipids and proteins in lipoproteins and their role in atherosclerosis</h2><div id="lipid_athero.Overview"><h3>Overview</h3><p>The response to retention hypothesis for the initiation of atherosclerosis posits that retention of LDL in the artery wall leads to its modification into highly atherogenic particles that initiate inflammatory responses. A key overall point is that retention of LDL leads to oxidative modification of LDL, allowing this oxidized LDL (oxLDL) to be recognized by scavenger receptors on macrophages and other cells. Uptake of oxLDL by macrophages leads to marked accumulation of cholesterol, converting them to foam cells and initiating development of atherosclerotic lesions. In addition to serving as a substrate for cholesterol accumulation, oxLDL exerts a wide range of bioactivities that are consistent with it being critical for driving atherogenesis (<a href="/sites/books/NBK343489/table/lipid_athero.T.potential_atherogenic_act/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroTpotentialatherogenicact">Table 1</a>). In mouse models, loss of enzymes that modulate LDL oxidation increases atherosclerosis, and dietary antioxidants that reduce levels of oxLDL also inhibit atherosclerosis. Although human trials with dietary antioxidants have failed to reduce disease outcomes, it is important to recognize that these interventions are less efficacious in reducing oxLDL levels in humans than in rodent models. Additional studies are needed to determine optimal interventions for lowering oxLDL levels and whether such interventions will be effective for preventing or treating atherosclerosis.</p><div id="lipid_athero.T.potential_atherogenic_act" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 1</span></h3><div class="caption"><p>-Potential Atherogenic Activities of Oxidized LDL (oxLDL)</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK343489/table/lipid_athero.T.potential_atherogenic_act/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__lipid_athero.T.potential_atherogenic_act_lrgtbl__"><table><tbody><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<b>Macrophages</b>
</td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<b>Smooth muscle cells</b>
</td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Serves as ligand for recognition by scavenger receptors <sup>256, 257, 258</sup></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Induces proliferation, migration, and transition to inflammatory phenotype <sup>276, 277, 278, 279</sup></td></tr><tr><td scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Serves as substrate for unregulated cholesterol uptake <sup>262</sup></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;"></td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Induces expression and secretion of inflammatory cytokines <sup>280, 281, 282, 283</sup></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<b>Lymphocytes</b>
</td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Induces polarization to M1 (minimally oxidized LDL) or M2-phenotype (highly oxidized LDL) <sup>284</sup></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Serves as a neo-antigen <sup>274</sup></td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Inhibits egress from atherosclerotic lesions <sup>289</sup></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Induces chemotaxis <sup>275</sup></td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Induces macrophage apoptosis and rupture of atherosclerotic plaques <sup>290, 291, 292</sup></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Increases antibody production <sup>275</sup></td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;"></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<b>Other cell types</b>
</td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<b>Endothelial cells</b>
</td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Induces chemotaxis of monocytes, PMN, and eosinophils <sup>285, 286, 287, 288</sup></td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Induces surface expression of adhesion molecules <sup>266, 268, 269, 270</sup></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Increases platelet aggregation <sup>293, 294, 295, 296</sup></td></tr><tr><td scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Induces inflammatory genes including cytokine release <sup>271, 272</sup></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Activates dendritic cells and induces their release of T cells stimulating cytokines <sup>284</sup></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.Peroxidation_of_Polyunsatur"><h3>Peroxidation of Polyunsaturated Fatty Acids Generates Oxidatively Modified Lipoproteins</h3><p>The outer shell of lipoproteins is composed of phospholipids with polyunsaturated fatty acid (PUFA) side chains. These PUFAs (and to a lesser extent the PUFAs of cholesterol esters and triglycerides in the lipoprotein core) are highly vulnerable to oxidation by free radical species, particularly hydroxyl radicals (<sup>&#x025cf;</sup>OH). This vulnerability results from the relatively low energy required for free radicals to abstract hydrogen atoms located between two adjacent double bonds (bis-allelic hydrogens). Hydrogen abstraction by free radicals creates a lipid radical that reacts nearly instantaneously with any molecular oxygen present in the environment.</p><p>The resulting lipid peroxide radical (LOO<sup>&#x025cf;</sup>) can then propagate the radical reaction by abstracting hydrogens from neighboring phospholipids or can react with itself to create a large number of secondary peroxidation products (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F7/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF7" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF7">Figure 7</a>). Secondary products that may be relevant to atherogenesis can be thought of in two broad classes: oxidized lipids (primarily oxidized phospholipids but also oxidized cholesterol esters) and reactive lipid aldehydes that exert their effects by modifying proteins and other macromolecules. Oxidized phospholipids (oxPL) include chain shortened oxPL such as 1-palmitoyl-2-oxovaleroyl-sn-glycero-3-phosphorylcholine (POVPC)(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.242">242</a>), 1-O-alkyl-2-azelaoyl-sn-glycero-3-phophorylcholine (azPAF) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.243">243</a>), and 1-(Palmitoyl)-2-(5-keto-6-octene-dioyl) phosphatidylcholine (KOdiA-PC) and cyclized oxPL such as 1-palmitoyl-2-(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.5">5</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.6">6</a>)-epoxyisoprostane E2-sn-glycero-3-phosphocholine (PEIPC) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.244">244</a>) and 1-palmitoyl-2-F2-isoprostane-sn-glycero-3-phosphocholine (F2IsoP-PC) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.245">245</a>). Reactive lipid species include malondialdehyde (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.246">246</a>), 4-hydroxynonenal (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.246">246</a>), and isolevuglandins (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.247">247</a>) that modify proteins associated with lipoprotein particles including ApoB100 (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F7/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF7" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF7">Figure 7</a>).</p><div id="lipid_athero.F7" class="figure bk_fig"><div class="graphic"><img src="/sites/books/NBK343489/bin/lipid_athero-Image007.jpg" alt="Figure 7. . Oxidation of Phospholipid Polyunsaturated Fatty Acids." /></div><h3><span class="label">Figure 7. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Oxidation of Phospholipid Polyunsaturated Fatty Acids. Oxidation of phospholipids containing polyunsaturated fatty acids present in plasma lipoproteins results in formation of a variety of reactive lipid aldehydes and oxidized phospholipids that convert these lipoproteins to atherogenic particles. Reactive lipid species include malondialdehyde (MDA), isolevuglandins (IsoLG), methyglyoxal (MGO), 4-oxononenal (ONE), and 4-hydroxynonenal (HNE). Oxidized phospholipids include 1-palmitoyl-2-oxovaleroyl-sn-glycero-3-phosphorylcholine (POVPC), 1-O-alkyl-2-azelaoyl-sn-glycero-3-phophorylcholine (azPAF), 1-(Palmitoyl)-2-(5-keto-6-octene-dioyl) phosphatidylcholine (KOdiA-PC), 1-palmitoyl-2-F2-isoprostane-sn-glycero-3-phosphocholine (F2IsoP-PC), and 1-palmitoyl-2-(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.5">5</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.6">6</a>)-epoxyisoprostane E2-sn-glycero-3-phosphocholine (PEIPC).</p></div></div><p>It is critical to keep in mind that oxidatively modified LDLs (oxLDLs) are in fact highly heterogeneous and complex particles, even though oxLDL is usually referred to as a discrete entity. Oxidation of LDL in vitro has been used extensively to study the biological activities of oxLDL, but, even here, the actual species present varies significantly based on the oxidation method (exposure to air, to copper, or to oxidases) and length of oxidation. Many of the methods commonly used to measure the concentration of oxLDL in vivo only measure general characteristics of oxLDL. For instance, because the reaction of reactive lipid species with lysine residues of ApoB100 converts LDL from a positively charged particle to a negatively charged particle, oxLDL is often detected by increased mobility during agarose gel electrophoresis. Alternatively, oxLDL in plasma and other tissues can be quantified by the immunoreactivity of the natural IgM autoantibody E06. However, while E06 recognizes a variety of oxidized phosphatidylcholines, it does not necessarily recognize all oxPL equally. Therefore, equivalent E06 immunoreactivity does not necessarily mean exposure to identical oxLDL particles. While modification of LDL with malondialdehyde (MDA-LDL) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.248">248</a>) is often used as a model of oxLDL for bioactivity assays, modification of LDL by other reactive lipid species can exert unique effects from MDA-LDL, and MDA-LDL does not include any of the various oxPL species. Therefore, it is important to keep in mind that in vivo oxLDL is a mixture of many different compounds and that the atherogenic activities of oxLDL represent the net cellular responses to the full range of compounds present.</p><p>While oxLDL has been studied in greatest detail, all lipoproteins are vulnerable to oxidation at least in vitro, and this oxidative modification alters their biological activities in ways that may be atherogenic. The species of plasma lipoprotein that has the highest content of oxidized phospholipids (oxPL) depends on the species of oxPL under consideration. This suggests that not all oxPL are formed in situ on the lipoprotein where they are found and might instead be transferred from other lipoproteins or tissues. Lp(a) is the major carrier in plasma of oxPLs that are detected by E06 immunoreactivity (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.249">249</a>) and these oxPLs associate with Lp(a) in preference to native LDL particles in human plasma (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.250">250</a>). E06 immunoreactive oxPL generated in chemically oxidized LDL can rapidly transfer to Lp(a) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.249">249</a>), so the high content of these lipids in Lp(a) isolated from human plasma may be due either to direct oxidation of Lp(a) or by transfer of the oxPL from oxLDL to Lp(a). In LDL and Lp(a) isolated from human plasma, levels of MDA-modified lysine (based on E014 immunoreactivity) are higher in LDL than Lp(a), while E06 immunoreactivity is much greater for Lp(a) than for LDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.249">249</a>). Because MDA-modified proteins do not readily transfer between particles, these findings suggest that oxidation initially occurs in LDL with subsequent transfer of oxPL to Lp(a). Thus, a physiological role has been proposed for Lp(a) in binding and transporting oxPL in the plasma (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.251">251</a>). Unlike oxPL detected by E06 immunoreactivity that are highest in Lp(a), the F<sub>2</sub>-IsoP-phospholipids forms of oxPL are highest in HDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.252">252</a>). As with Lp(a), the high levels of these oxPLs in HDL may well be the result of transfer from other oxidized lipoproteins and tissues. Because oxidation is unlikely to occur in the circulation, the rate that oxPL are transferred from tissue to various plasma lipoproteins could potentially be an important determinant of the risk for atherosclerosis.</p><p>Significant correlations have been found between levels of oxLDL and extent of atherosclerosis in human patients. Measurement of oxLDL using E06 antibody showed that: 1) significant elevation of oxLDL in acute coronary syndromes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.250">250</a>), 2) treatment with a statin markedly reduced these levels (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.253">253</a>), 3) oxLDL levels are higher in children with familial hypercholesterolemia compared to their siblings (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.254">254</a>), and 4) oxLDL levels predict the presence and progression of atherosclerosis and symptomatic cardiovascular disease (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.255">255</a>). Measurement of oxLDL using antibodies against MDA-LDL found that oxLDL were elevated in patients with coronary artery disease (CAD) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.256">256</a>), that elevated levels of oxLDL predicted future cardiac events in diabetic patients with CAD, that oxLDL were particularly elevated in patients with rheumatoid arthritis and CAD compared to either alone (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.257">257</a>), and that treatment with fibrates decreased levels of oxLDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.258">258</a>). Thus, there is a clear correlation between the presence of oxLDL and cardiovascular disease.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Mechanisms_of_Lipoprotein_O"><h3>Mechanisms of Lipoprotein Oxidation In Vitro And In Vivo</h3><p>The precise mechanisms that generate oxidized lipoproteins in vivo are still only partially understood. LDL circulating in the plasma appears to be protected from oxidation, both by dietary antioxidants such as vitamin E and C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.259">259</a>) and by protective enzymes including glutathione peroxidases (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.260">260</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.261">261</a>), peroxiredoxins, PAF-acetylhydrolase (also known as lipoprotein-PLA2) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.262">262</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.263">263</a>), and paraoxonases (PON) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.264">264</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.265">265</a>). Penetration of LDL into the artery wall occurs at branch points in the aorta and other places with turbulent flow and shear stress. Retention of LDL in the intima, due to interactions with extracellular matrix such as chondroitin sulfate-rich proteoglycans, sequesters LDL away from the antioxidant environment of the plasma and exposes LDL to oxidation. A variety of oxidases and peroxidases generate strong oxidants that can readily oxidize LDL. These include myeloperoxidase (MPO) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.266">266</a>), xanthine oxidase (XO) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.267">267</a>), NADPH oxidases (NOXs) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.268">268</a>), and inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.269">269</a>). Oxygenases such as lipoxygenases (LOX) have also been shown to oxidize LDL in vitro (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.270">270</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.271">271</a>). The extent that each of these enzymes contributes to lipoprotein oxidation in vivo and thus to atherosclerosis remains to be fully elucidated, and there is much we do not understand about these individual processes. This is illustrated by studies on MPO and the 12/15-Lipoxygenase, the two enzymes most closely linked to lipoprotein oxidation.</p><div id="lipid_athero.MYELOPEROXIDASE_MPO"><h4>MYELOPEROXIDASE (MPO)</h4><p>MPO released from activated neutrophils (and to a lesser extent from monocytes/macrophages) can accumulate in the subintimal space of the artery wall(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.272">272</a>), so neutrophil activation indirectly increases the chance for lipoprotein oxidation. Increased plasma levels of MPO correlate with increased levels of oxLDL in hypercholesterolemic children (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.273">273</a>). Increased MPO blood levels also associate with increased risk for atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.274">274</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.277">277</a>) and polymorphisms in the MPO gene that lower MPO activity reduce the risk for atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.278">278</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.279">279</a>).</p><p>Incubation of lipoproteins with MPO generates oxidized phospholipids that serve as ligands for CD36(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.280">280</a>). A putative binding site for MPO with the apoB of LDL has been identified (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.281">281</a>), although further verification is needed. Of interest, MPO also associates with HDL via binding to ApoAI and PON1 in a ternary complex (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.282">282</a>), so that the binding of MPO to HDL and subsequent generation of reactive oxygen species may account for the high levels of oxidized lipids carried by HDL. Association of MPO with HDL leads to modification of tyrosine 71 of paraoxonase, reducing PON1 activity (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.282">282</a>). It also generates reactive lipid dicarbonyls such as isolevuglandins that modify ApoAI (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.283">283</a>) and phosphatidylethanolamine (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.284">284</a>). In the presence of small molecules that scavenge lipid dicarbonyls, the ability of MPO to crosslink ApoAI is markedly reduced (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.283">283</a>). Modification of HDL by lipid dicarbonyls such as isolevuglandins and MDA reduce its ability to drive cholesterol efflux from macrophages and protect against inflammatory stimuli such as LPS (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.283">283</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.285">285</a>).</p><p>Mouse models have been used to directly examine the contribution of MPO to atherosclerosis, although these studies carry the caveat that mouse MPO levels are only 10-20% that of humans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.286">286</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.287">287</a>). Transplantation of bone marrow from genetically altered mice into atherosclerosis susceptible strains (e.g. <i>Ldlr</i>
<sup>-/-</sup> and <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice) after lethal irradiation to ablate host hematopoietic cells is commonly used to study the effect of specific genes expressed by macrophages and other hematopoietic cells on atherogenesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.99">99</a>). Reconstitution of <i>Ldlr</i>
<sup>-/-</sup> mice with macrophages overexpressing MPO markedly increased their susceptibility to atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.288">288</a>). However, transplantation of MPO<sup>-/-</sup> macrophages into <i>Ldlr</i>
<sup>-/-</sup> mice, also markedly increased atherosclerosis, and this was confirmed in MPO<sup>-/-</sup>/ <i>Ldlr</i>
<sup>-/-</sup> double knockout mice compared to MPO<sup>+/+</sup>/ <i>Ldlr</i>
<sup>-/-</sup> controls (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.289">289</a>). The reasons for these paradoxical findings with both MPO overexpression and deletion remain unclear. Perhaps the complete lack of MPO activity is harmful because it allows overgrowth of specific microbes that incite atherosclerosis via alternative mechanisms. In contrast to effects of complete ablation, a recently developed selective MPO inhibitor (e.g. INV315) that only partially reduces MPO activity markedly reduced atherosclerosis in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.290">290</a>). Thus, clinical studies with selective MPO inhibitors are needed to determine if this will be a meaningful therapeutic approach to the treatment of atherosclerosis in humans.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.1215LIPOXYGENASES"><h4>12/15-LIPOXYGENASES</h4><p>Although the primary substrates for lipoxygenases are non-esterified fatty acids, exposure of LDL to 15-LOX also leads to oxidation of phospholipids and cholesterol esters (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.270">270</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.271">271</a>). In mice, the gene analogous to the human 15-LOX encodes a lipoxygenase that converts arachidonyl chains to both 12-HPETE and 15-HPETE and is thus a 12/15-LOX. 12/15-LOX<sup>-/-</sup> mice on <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> background have reduced atherosclerosis compared to <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.291">291</a>). Importantly, they also have lower levels of autoantibodies against oxLDL and MDA-LDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.291">291</a>). These results support the notion that 12/15-LOX can directly contribute to atherosclerosis via LDL oxidation. Nevertheless, the role of 15-LOX in human atherogenesis is less clear-cut. While homozygotes of an Alox15 variant that almost completely ablates 15-LOX activity tended to have a reduced risk for coronary artery disease, heterozygotes paradoxically have increased risk of disease (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.292">292</a>). Other polymorphisms in the Alox15 gene encoding 15-LOX increase risk for coronary artery calcification (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.293">293</a>), yet others have no effect (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.294">294</a>). Direct correlations between Alox15 polymorphisms and biochemical measurements of oxidized lipoproteins or oxPL and oxidized cholesterol esters have not been reported to date in humans, but are clearly needed.</p></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.Biological_Activities_of_Ox"><h3>Biological Activities of OxLDL And Receptors That Mediate These Activities</h3><p>Perhaps the most important atherogenic effect of LDL oxidation is that this modification of LDL shifts recognition and internalization of the lipoprotein from the LDL receptor (LDLR) to scavenger receptors (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.295">295</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.297">297</a>). While internalization of LDL by the LDLR in hepatocytes downregulates cholesterol synthesis to maintain cholesterol homeostasis, internalization of oxLDL by scavenger receptors fails to trigger this inhibition (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.298">298</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.299">299</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.300">300</a>). Thus, cholesterol synthesis continues unabated despite the fact that peripheral cells are accumulating large amounts of cholesterol. In particular, macrophages express scavenger receptors and gluttonously take up large quantities oxLDL to form foam cells in the initial atherosclerotic lesion (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.301">301</a>).</p><p>OxLDL also activates a number of cellular responses in macrophages, dendritic cells, endothelial cells, T cells, and smooth muscle cells that in aggregate promote inflammation, lesion formation, atherogenesis, and unstable atherosclerotic plaques (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.302">302</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.304">304</a>). OxLDL induces surface expression of adhesion molecules and the release of chemokines from endothelial cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.305">305</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.311">311</a>), all of which are important steps in recruitment of leukocytes to sites of lesions. Exposure to oxLDL activates dendritic cells so that they induce T-cell proliferation and production of IL-17 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.312">312</a>). OxLDL itself also serves as a neo-antigen (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.313">313</a>). OxLDL also induces increased antibody generation by lymphocytes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.314">314</a>). OxLDL also promotes smooth muscle cell proliferation, migration, and transition to a proinflammatory phenotype (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.315">315</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.318">318</a>). OxLDL induces secretion by macrophages of inflammatory cytokines (e.g. TNF&#x003b1;, IL-1, MCP-1, and IL-8) that activate other inflammatory cell types (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.319">319</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.322">322</a>). OxLDL polarizes macrophages towards the M1-like phenotype or M2-like phenotype depending on its extent of oxidation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.323">323</a>). OxLDL promotes the chemotaxis of monocytes, neutrophils, eosinophils, and T cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.314">314</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.324">324</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.327">327</a>), bringing them into the arterial wall. In contrast, oxLDL inhibits macrophage emigration out of atherosclerotic lesions, because it induces netrin-1 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.328">328</a>). OxLDL induces apoptosis of macrophages and development of unstable plaques prone to rupture (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.329">329</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.331">331</a>). Thrombotic arterial occlusion in the aftermath of plaque rupture is a critical cause of mortality, therefore the fact that oxLDL increases platelet aggregation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.332">332</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.335">335</a>) suggests an additional mechanism whereby elevated circulating oxLDL may increase risk of mortality during acute coronary events (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.336">336</a>). As discussed in detail below, identification of cognate receptors for various components of oxLDL and other oxidized lipoproteins has provided important insight into the mechanisms by which these oxidized lipoproteins exert their pathophysiological effects.</p><div id="lipid_athero.MACROPHAGE_SCAVENGER_RECEPT"><h4>MACROPHAGE SCAVENGER RECEPTOR (SR-AI)</h4><p>In 1979, Brown and Goldstein demonstrated that macrophages had specific binding sites for acetylated LDL (AcLDL) that allowed uptake of this modified LDL even in the presence of high cellular cholesterol levels (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.298">298</a>). This was in contrast to LDL uptake by the LDLR, which is markedly downregulated when cellular cholesterol levels rise (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF2" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF2">Figure 2</a>). Cholesterol synthesis is also downregulated by LDL uptake by LDLR (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.337">337</a>). The lack of feedback inhibition during uptake of modified LDL by this unidentified receptor suggested a plausible mechanism for the massive accumulation of cholesterol in macrophages that generates foam cells. The putative receptor mediating this binding was named the macrophage scavenger receptor (MSR). Later, oxLDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.338">338</a>) and MDA-LDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.248">248</a>) were shown to compete with AcLDL for binding and uptake by macrophages, suggesting they were native ligands for MSR. In 1990, Kodama et al. purified and sequenced this scavenger receptor, allowing identification of the <i>MSR</i> gene (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.339">339</a>). Through alternative gene splicing, this gene gives rise to Scavenger Receptor A&#x02013;I (SR-AI), SRA-II, and SRA-III. Deletion of the <i>MSR</i> gene in C57BL6 mice fed butterfat diet substantially reduced atherosclerotic lesions and deletion of MSR in <i>Ldlr<sup>-/-</sup></i> mice also reduced lesion formation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.340">340</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.CD36_AND_OTHER_SCAVENGER_RE"><h4>CD36 AND OTHER SCAVENGER RECEPTORS</h4><p>Subsequent work has shown that in addition to SR-AI, macrophages express a wide range of scavenger receptors that recognize oxidized lipoproteins including MARCO, scavenger receptor-B1, -B2, -B3 (CD36), and Lectin-like oxLDL Receptor-1 (LOX-1) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.341">341</a>). These scavenger receptors belong to a larger family of pattern recognition receptors, all of which are individually capable of binding to a wide spectrum of ligands. Quantitatively, SR-A1 and CD36 account for the vast majority of all oxLDL uptake by macrophages (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.342">342</a>). The specific ligands of the two receptors on oxLDL appear to diverge (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.342">342</a>). SR-AI appears to preferentially recognize more rigorously oxidized LDL and seems to primarily recognize modified lysine residues like MDA-lysines. In contrast, the primary ligands of CD36 on oxLDL appear to be oxidized phospholipids, in particular fragmented phosphatidylcholine including azPAF (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.243">243</a>), POVPC (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.343">343</a>) and KOdiA-PC (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.280">280</a>). <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice lacking CD36 are more vulnerable to some bacterial infections (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.344">344</a>) but also have less atherosclerosis when fed a high cholesterol diet (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.345">345</a>).</p><p>Recent findings suggest that SR-AI and other scavenger receptors have both pro- and anti-atherosclerotic effects, depending on the context. For instance, deletion of the MSR gene actually increased lesion size in male <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.346">346</a>); however, deletion of both MSR and CD36 greatly reduces lesion complexity and vulnerable plaques, the most critical aspect of lesion development (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.347">347</a>). The complex results of scavenger receptor deletion should not be surprising given that scavenger receptors have multiple ligands and that an important role of scavenger receptors expressed by macrophages is to allow these macrophages to remove bacteria and damaged cells from surrounding tissues. Under normal physiological conditions, uptake of oxLDL by macrophages is probably generally protective, because subsequent efflux of the cholesterol from the macrophages to HDL via reverse cholesterol transport as well as emigration of these macrophages from the arterial wall to lymph nodes serves to minimize the accumulation of cholesterol-laden macrophages in the arterial wall. However, under conditions where reverse cholesterol transport capacity is reduced or where emigration of macrophages is inhibited, uptake of oxLDL by macrophages leads to its accumulation and initiation of pathophysiological processes.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.TOLLLIKE_RECEPTORS_AND_OTHE"><h4>TOLL-LIKE RECEPTORS AND OTHER TARGETS OF OXLDL</h4><p>In addition to scavenger receptors, other pattern recognition receptors also recognize components of oxLDL. Perhaps most important among these are the Toll-like Receptors (TLR) including TLR-2 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.348">348</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.349">349</a>), TLR-4 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.350">350</a>), TLR-6 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.351">351</a>), TLR-7 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.352">352</a>), and TLR-9 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.352">352</a>). TLRs can interact with scavenger receptors, for instance, CD36 forms complexes with TLR4 and TLR6 that recognize oxLDL and activate NFkappaB (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.351">351</a>). While bacterial components such as bacterial lipopolysaccharide (LPS) are full agonists for TLRs, oxLDL components like POVPC often appear to act functionally as partial agonists of TLRs, so that activation of macrophages and dendritic cells by full agonists like LPS is reduced in the presence of oxLDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.353">353</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.354">354</a>).</p><p>In addition to TLRs, another important pattern recognition receptor for oxLDL is the receptor for advanced glycation end-products (RAGE) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.355">355</a>). Other factors of the innate immune response that bind oxidized phospholipids including C-reactive protein(CRP) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.356">356</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.357">357</a>) and natural IgM antibodies like E06 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.358">358</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.359">359</a>). While scavenger and pattern recognition receptors tend to recognize broad classes of compounds, a number of G-protein coupled receptors (GPCRs) recognize specific oxidized phospholipids. These include the receptor for platelet-activating factor (PAFR) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.360">360</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.362">362</a>), prostaglandin receptor EP2 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.363">363</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.364">364</a>), and sphingosine-1-phosphate receptor 1 (S1P1) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.365">365</a>). Intracellular receptors for oxidized phospholipids include nuclear hormone receptors PPAR alpha (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.366">366</a>) and PPAR gamma (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.243">243</a>). Non-receptor, intracellular targets for oxLDL include c-SRC (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.367">367</a>) and NRF-2 (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.368">368</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.369">369</a>).</p></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.Mechanisms_Protecting_Again"><h3>Mechanisms Protecting Against LDL Oxidation In Vivo</h3><p>Given the susceptibility of LDL to oxidation, it is perhaps not surprising that a number of mechanisms appear to exist in order to protect LDL from oxidation. These include small molecule antioxidants circulating in plasma and enzymes that catabolize oxidized lipids. How essential each of these mechanisms are to the control of oxLDL levels and preventing the development of atherosclerosis remains an area of active investigation. Obviously, a better understanding of the relationship between changes in protective mechanism and atherogenesis might allow identification of particularly vulnerable individuals and the development of novel therapeutic approaches.</p><div id="lipid_athero.SMALL_MOLECUE_ANTIOXIDANTS"><h4>SMALL MOLECUE ANTIOXIDANTS</h4><p>Circulating small molecule antioxidants such as ascorbate (vitamin C), alpha-tocopherol (vitamin E), urate, and bilirubin serve as sacrificial targets reacting with free radicals and reactive oxygen species to prevent lipid and protein oxidation. Thus, even when strong oxidants are added to plasma ex vivo, there is relatively little generation of oxLDL until the oxidants have depleted these small molecule antioxidants, most specifically ascorbate (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.370">370</a>). Depletion of vitamin C and vitamin E increase atherosclerosis in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup>mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.371">371</a>). Importantly, plasma ascorbate levels inversely correlate with prevalence of cardiovascular disease in humans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.372">372</a>). Supplementation with vitamin C appears to play a role in preventing endothelial dysfunction in humans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.373">373</a>). However, it is not clear that supplementing dietary antioxidants beyond those typically obtained in a well-balanced diet endows any additional atheroprotective effects. Supplementation with dietary antioxidants inhibits development of atherosclerosis in susceptible mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.374">374</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.378">378</a>). While a few human trials with dietary antioxidants have demonstrated reduced atherosclerosis and cardiovascular disease (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.379">379</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.382">382</a>), most large-scale trials have failed to demonstrate any disease reduction (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.383">383</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.387">387</a>). The reasons underlying these failures continue to be investigated and debated (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.388">388</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.389">389</a>). Because it had not been fully appreciated that relatively high doses of these antioxidants were needed to markedly alter lipid peroxidation rates in humans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.390">390</a>), one possibility is that the doses used in most large scale prevention trials were simply insufficient (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.390">390</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.391">391</a>) . However, the ability to use very high doses of small molecule antioxidants like vitamin E for extended periods of times may be limited by the toxicity of these high doses (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.392">392</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.ANTIOXIDANT_ENZYMES"><h4>ANTIOXIDANT ENZYMES</h4><p>Antioxidant enzymes appear to play a more critical role than dietary antioxidants in limiting lipoprotein oxidation. Two families of nonheme peroxidases, the glutathione peroxidases and the peroxiredoxins, appear to be the most critical. Glutathione peroxidases (Gpx) 1-4 are selenoproteins that convert glutathione to glutathione disulfide while reducing peroxides (including lipid peroxides) to water (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.393">393</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.394">394</a>). Polymorphisms in glutathione peroxidase 1 (Gpx1) are associated with increased risk for atherosclerosis in various human populations (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.395">395</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.397">397</a>). Furthermore, genetic deletion of Gpx1 markedly exacerbates atherosclerosis in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.398">398</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.399">399</a>), while overexpression of Gpx4 in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice inhibits atherogenesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.400">400</a>). Peroxiredoxins (Prdx) are cysteine containing proteins where the cysteine is oxidized to sulfenic acid during reduction of peroxides (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.401">401</a>). Deletion of either Prdx1 or Prdx2 increases atherosclerosis in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.402">402</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.403">403</a>). Overexpression of Prdx4 inhibits atherosclerosis in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.404">404</a>). In contrast, overexpression of Prdx6 failed to inhibit atherosclerosis in C57BL6 mice fed an atherogenic diet (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.405">405</a>).</p><p>In general, studies looking for associations between risk for atherosclerosis and polymorphisms or deficiencies in other major antioxidant genes including catalase, SOD-1, -2, and -3, and glutathione S-transferase have been negative (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.406">406</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.407">407</a>). In fact, SOD-1 overexpression may even increase fatty streak lesions in mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.408">408</a>). However, SOD-1 does inhibit proliferation and migration of smooth muscle cells induced by oxLDL in vitro (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.315">315</a>), and overexpression of both SOD-1 and catalase reduce atherosclerosis in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.409">409</a>). <i>Sod2</i><sup>+/-</sup> mice crossed with <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice have increased atherosclerosis compared to control <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.410">410</a>), but there is little effect on atherosclerosis of crossing <i>Sod3</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice with <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.411">411</a>). Several studies have demonstrated an association between <i>SOD2</i> and hypertriglyceridemia (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.412">412</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.413">413</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.ENZYMES_THAT_CATABOLIZE_LIP"><h4>ENZYMES THAT CATABOLIZE LIPIDS</h4><p>In addition to anti-oxidant enzymes, several enzymes specifically catabolize oxidized phospholipids including secreted Platelet-Activating Factor Hydrolases (sPAF-AH) and Paraoxonases (PON). sPAF-AH, also known as lipoprotein associated PLA2 (LP-PLA2) is a calcium independent PLA<sub>2</sub> secreted by macrophages that primarily circulates on LDL and to a lesser extent on HDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.414">414</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.415">415</a>). sPAF-AH does not hydrolyze phospholipids with the typical long-chain fatty acids, but efficiently cleaves phospholipids with oxidatively fragmented (e.g. azPAF and POVPC) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.362">362</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.416">416</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.417">417</a>) or oxidatively cyclized (e.g. F2-isoprostane-PC) sn-2 chains (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.418">418</a>). Whether this effect results in a net gain of pro- or anti-inflammatory lipids is controversial, because only some of these oxPL are highly potent inflammatory mediators, while others are partial agonists that might therefore antagonize inflammatory responses to other mediators like LPS. Furthermore, this hydrolysis generates lysoPC and lysoPAF, which are proinflammatory at high concentrations. This ambivalent effect is also seen in vivo. While a large number of clinical studies have found that increased sPAF-AH predicts increased risk for atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.419">419</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.420">420</a>), whether increased sPAF-AH actually contributes to atherogenesis or simply reflects a compensatory increase in response to elevated oxLDL is unclear (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.421">421</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.422">422</a>). Some gene polymorphisms in sPAF-AH that reduce its activity (i.e. Val279Phe) appear to increase the risk of myocardial infarction (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.423">423</a>), yet another polymorphism (i.e. Ala379Val) appears to have little effect (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.424">424</a>). The interpretation that increased sPAF-AH activity caused an increased risk of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD) led to the development of selective sPAF-AH inhibitors and their clinical trials (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.425">425</a>). However, two recently completed phase III trials with one such inhibitor, darapladib, found that while this drug significantly reduced circulating PAF-AH activity, it had no effect on ASCVD events (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.426">426</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.427">427</a>).</p><p>Paraoxonases (PONs) were originally named for their ability to hydrolyze the neurotoxin paraoxon and this activity is still routinely used to assay paraoxonase activity in plasma. However, in terms of atherosclerosis, the most important physiological function of PONs appears to be their ability to protect against LDL oxidation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.428">428</a>). PON-1 and PON-3 circulate bound to HDL. HDL treated with specific inhibitors of PON fails to protect LDL from oxidation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.429">429</a>). Treatment of oxLDL with purified PON1 markedly decreases its ability to induce endothelial cell activation and monocyte binding (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.264">264</a>). Genetic deletion of PON1 markedly increases atherosclerosis in C57BL6 mice(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.430">430</a>) , and this is further exacerbated in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.431">431</a>). Conversely, overexpression of PON-1 reduces atherosclerotic lesions in both wild-type mice fed high cholesterol diets and <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.432">432</a>). Adenovirus expression of PON-2 and PON-3 also inhibits atherosclerosis in <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.433">433</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.434">434</a>), indicating that all three PON enzymes have protective effects. However, transgenic <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice overexpressing the entire gene cluster of PON genes (PON-1, -2, -3) were not further protected compared to <i>Apoe</i><sup>-/-</sup> mice with transgenic expression of PON-1 or PON-3 alone (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.435">435</a>), suggesting these effects are redundant rather than additive. These mouse studies appear relevant to human disease, as a large number of studies have shown that polymorphisms in PON1 are associated with increased risk for atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.265">265</a>). It should be noted that PON activity varies greatly even in persons with the same polymorphism, suggesting that environmental factors leading to PON inactivation may also be important in determining disease risk.</p></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.Summary_for_Oxidized_Lipopr"><h3>Summary for Oxidized Lipoproteins</h3><p>In summary, substantial evidence has accumulated over the past several decades for a causative role for oxidized lipoproteins in the initiation and progression of atherosclerosis and the need to reduce lipoprotein oxidation in order to reduce disease burden. Nevertheless, significant questions remain including which mechanisms are most important for driving lipoprotein oxidation, what treatment strategies can effectively reduce lipoprotein oxidation, and what are the key components of oxidized lipoproteins that drive atherogenesis?</p></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.ELEVATED_LDLC_AND_RISK_FOR"><h2 id="_lipid_athero_ELEVATED_LDLC_AND_RISK_FOR_">ELEVATED LDL-C AND RISK FOR ASCVD</h2><div id="lipid_athero.Genetic_Causes_of_Elevated"><h3>Genetic Causes of Elevated LDL-C</h3><p>As described above, FH is an autosomal dominant inherited disorder associated with elevated levels of LDL-C and premature ASCVD, and provides some of the most compelling evidence for a causal role for LDL-C in atherosclerosis. Brown and Goldstein discovered the LDLR pathway and found that mutations in the <i>Ldlr</i> gene cause FH (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.300">300</a>). Heterozygotes for loss-of-function mutations have cholesterol levels that are about twice normal, and these subjects are at increased risk of premature CVE. In contrast, individuals with homozygous FH have extremely high levels of LDL-C (&#x0003e; 500 mg/dL) and often develop severe coronary atherosclerosis and supravalvular aortic stenosis in early childhood. The prevalence of heterozygous FH is around 1/200-250 in the USA, whereas homozygous FH is extremely rare affecting only about 1/160,000 to 1/250,000 individuals (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.436">436</a>). Nonetheless, about 20% of people having myocardial infarctions (MI) before 40 years of age have heterozygous FH. Thus, FH offers an important opportunity to target therapies to prevent atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.437">437</a>), but FH remains under recognized with recent evidence suggesting that only 1-10% of subjects with FH have been identified (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.438">438</a>). Most individuals with significant hypercholesterolemia do not have classic monogenic autosomal dominant inherited dyslipidemias, but polygenic factors contributing to susceptibility to environmental factors underlie the observed increase in LDL-C levels. A recent study suggests that among individuals with LDL cholesterol &#x02265;190 mg/dl, gene sequencing identified a monogenic FH mutation in only &#x0003c;2%of subjects (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.439">439</a>). However, for any observed LDL cholesterol, FH mutation carriers are at substantially increased risk for CAD (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.439">439</a>). Pathogenic variants in three genes (LDLR, APOB, and PCSK9) account for the majority of monogenic FH cases. Recent genome-wide association studies (GWAS) have identified more than 50 discrete genetic loci that are associated with an increased risk of CVE(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.440">440</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.441">441</a>). Many of these genetic loci are associated with genes previously known to impact LDL-C levels and cardiovascular risk (e.g. <i>Ldlr</i>, <i>APOB</i>, <i>PCSK9</i>), but novel loci that impact both LDL-C levels and risk for MI have also been identified, e.g. sortilin-1 (<i>SORT1</i>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.442">442</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.443">443</a>). Most importantly, inherited low levels of LDL-C due to loss-of-function mutations in the <i>PCSK9</i> gene have been shown to be associated with dramatic reductions in risk for ASCVD events in the Atherosclerosis Risk in Communities study (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.444">444</a>). Hence, genetic disorders of lipoprotein metabolism provide strong evidence that the impact of LDL-C on the development of atherosclerosis is dose- and time-dependent (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.445">445</a>), supporting a causal role for LDL-C in atherosclerosis.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Lowering_LDLC_Reduces_ASCVD"><h3>Lowering LDL-C Reduces ASCVD</h3><p>Large randomized outcomes trials of cholesterol lowering drugs have provided critical proof of the cholesterol hypothesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.446">446</a>). The Coronary Drug Project, conducted between 1966 and 1975, found niacin treatment showed modest benefit in decreasing definite nonfatal recurrent myocardial infarction by 26% (10.2% for niacin group vs 13.8% for placebo group) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.447">447</a>). However, there was no benefit in primary endpoint, total mortality. Impressively, with a mean follow-up of 15 years, nearly 9 years after termination of the trial, all-cause mortality was 11% lower in niacin group than in the placebo group (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.448">448</a>). The Lipid Clinics Research trial was another early major outcomes trial to show that lowering cholesterol reduced cardiovascular events. Treatment with cholestyramine, a bile acid binding inhibitor, resulted in a 12% reduction in LDL-C levels and a 19% reduction in CHD events (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.449">449</a>). The early lipid lowering cardiovascular outcomes trials were limited by a lack of highly effective approaches for lowering LDL-C levels, and several trials raised concerns that cholesterol lowering did not reduce total mortality and might increase the risk of cancer, accidental death and suicide (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.446">446</a>). The advent of the statin drug class (HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors) provided a much more effective approach to lowering LDL-C and laid to rest the concerns raised by the earlier trials. The 4S trial was a landmark clinical trial of cholesterol lowering with simvastatin in patients with coronary artery disease (CAD) and severely elevated levels of LDL-C that was designed to look at total mortality as the primary endpoint (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.450">450</a>). The 4S showed for the first time that lowering LDL-C levels by 35% with simvastatin resulted in a 30% reduction in total mortality with a 42% reduction in CHD deaths and a 34% reduction in the risk of Major Coronary Events (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.450">450</a>). A large number of subsequent trials extended these results to populations with CHD with low levels of LDL-C and to subjects without known CAD (primary prevention) with high or low levels of LDL-C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.451">451</a>). It is important to note that the relationship between on-treatment LDL-C lowering and reduction in cardiovascular events in secondary prevention trials was similar for both statin and non-statin approaches to lowering LDL-C levels. A large meta-analysis of 26 statin trials involving over 170,000 subjects demonstrated that statin treatment for 5&#x02013;years reduced the combined incidence of major coronary events, coronary revascularization, and stroke by 20% per every 1 mmol/l (38.7 mg/dL) reduction in LDL-C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.452">452</a>). These results have been extended by a recent large meta-analysis of 49 trials involving 9 different interventions to lower LDL that included more than 300,000 patients and approximately 40,000 major vascular events, each 1mmol/l (38.7mg/dl) reduction in LDL-C was associated with 23% relative reduction in the risk of major vascular events (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.453">453</a>). This raised the question of whether further lipid lowering would be of additional benefit. With the recent development of proprotein convertase subtilisin/kexin type 9 (PCSK9) inhibitors, dramatic additional LDL lowering up to 50 -70% is now possible. In the FOURIER trial, patients with prior stable CAD who received the PCSK9 inhibitor, evolocumab, in combination with statin therapy achieved median LDL-C levels of 30 mg/dl. This was associated with a 15% reduction in the composite endpoint of cardiovascular death, MI, stroke, hospitalization for unstable angina or coronary revascularization (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.454">454</a>). Similarly, ODYSSEY demonstrated that administration of alirocumab to acute coronary syndrome patients already on maximally tolerated statin therapy led to LDL-C values &#x0003c;50 mg/dl and was associated with a 15% reduction in the composite endpoint of death from coronary heart disease, nonfatal MI, ischemic stroke or unstable angina requiring hospitalization, and this benefit approached 24% in the subgroup of patients with initial LDL-C values &#x0003e;100 mg/dL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.455">455</a>). Together, the results of these PCSK9 trials reinforce the &#x0201c;lower is better&#x0201d; hypothesis.</p><p>Although statins are very effective in preventing CVE, many patients on statins do still have CVE, a phenomenon referred to as residual risk (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.456">456</a>). This residual risk is likely attributable at least in part to inflammation. Indeed, definitive support for this hypothesis recently came from the Canakinumab Anti-inflammatory Thrombosis Outcome Study (CANTOS), where administration of an anti-IL1&#x003b2; antibody to patients with prior MI and elevated serum hsCRP successfully reduced recurrent CVE independent of lipid lowering (&#x0003e;90% of patients were receiving concurrent statin therapy) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.457">457</a>). A secondary analysis of the FOURIER trial also demonstrated that, while relative risk for the primary cardiovascular endpoint was consistent across groups, the absolute risk reduction with evolocumab was greatest in patients with elevated hsCRP (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.458">458</a>). These results suggest that targeting both LDL and inflammation will provide the most robust strategy for lowering ASCVD risk.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Levels_of_LDLC_ApoB100_NonH"><h3>Levels of LDL-C, ApoB-100, Non-HDL Cholesterol and LDL-P as Markers for ASCVD Risk.</h3><p>Based on the strength of the direct association of LDL-C levels and risk for ASCVD, the guidelines for treatment of hypercholesterolemia have focused on LDL-C levels for risk assessment, stratification and treatment recommendations. Indeed, the terms LDL-C and LDL are often, though incorrectly, used interchangeably in practice. It is important to understand that LDL is a collection of particles defined by density (d = 1.019 &#x02013; 1.063 g/ml) that are heterogeneous, consisting of a large variety of lipids and proteins (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.459">459</a>). In addition, LDL particles vary in size and cholesterol content. The relationship between LDL-C levels and risk for ASCVD is &#x0201c;J-shaped&#x0201d;, and the predictive value of LDL-C levels is better at higher levels of LDL-C. Surprisingly, the majority of subjects presenting to the hospital with acute coronary artery syndrome do not have elevated levels of LDL-C, but tend to have low levels of HDL-C and elevated triglycerides (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.460">460</a>). There has been tremendous interest in whether other measures of LDL, including subpopulations, apoB100, or particle number, might serve as a better predictors of CVE than quantifying LDL cholesterol content.</p><p>Groundbreaking studies by Krauss and co-workers (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.461">461</a>) described two major patterns for LDL subpopulations based on size and density of the LDL particles. Pattern A is characterized by large buoyant LDL (lbLDL) particles, whereas Pattern B is associated with small dense LDL (sdLDL). Importantly, sdLDL is associated with increased triglyceride levels and low HDL-C, which is referred to as the lipid triad, a phenotype common in insulin resistance. Hence, Pattern B is commonly seen in subjects with obesity, metabolic syndrome and type 2 diabetes mellitus. A number of studies have reported that Pattern B is associated with an increased risk of CVE (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.462">462</a>). Several different approaches have been used to characterize LDL phenotypes, including gradient gel electrophoresis, ultracentrifugation (sequential and vertical), ion mobility and nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.462">462</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.463">463</a>). A number of mechanisms have been proposed to underlie the proatherogenic properties of sdLDL, including increased susceptibility of oxidation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.464">464</a>) and glycation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.465">465</a>), promoting arterial retention and increased macrophage foam cell formation. Cholesteryl ester transfer protein (CETP), which transfers CE from HDL to VLDL/LDL and triglycerides in the opposite direction, and hepatic lipase, which hydrolyses triglycerides, impacts the lipid composition and size of sdLDL. As such, increased levels of sdLDL have the potential to provide additional information regarding risk of CVE in individuals with normal LDL-C levels but elevated triglycerides and low HDL. Alternatively, it has been proposed that the real impact of sdLDL is due to increased LDL particle number.</p><p>Each LDL particle contains one molecule of apoB100, and the majority of apoB100 in plasma is on LDL particles (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.466">466</a>). Hence, levels of apoB100 correlate directly with LDL particle (LDL-P) number. A large number of studies have shown that levels of apoB100 are superior markers of ASCVD risk compared to LDL-C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.467">467</a>). Because the mass of cholesterol in LDL particles varies, LDL-C levels will result in overestimation apoB levels and the number of LDL particles, when LDL particles are cholesterol-enriched (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F8/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF8" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF8">Figure 8</a>) and underestimate apoB and LDL particle number when the particles are cholesterol depleted (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F8/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF8" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF8">Figure 8</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.468">468</a>).</p><div id="lipid_athero.F8" class="figure bk_fig"><div class="graphic"><img src="/sites/books/NBK343489/bin/lipid_athero-Image008.jpg" alt="Figure 8. . Schematic of the Relationship Between Measurements of LDL-C Versus ApoB100 Particle Number in Concordant and Discordant Human Populations." /></div><h3><span class="label">Figure 8. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Schematic of the Relationship Between Measurements of LDL-C Versus ApoB100 Particle Number in Concordant and Discordant Human Populations. Subjects with hypertriglyceridemia have enhanced numbers of small dense LDL where each particle is enriched in triglyceride (TG) and relatively poor in cholesteryl ester (CE) content compared to normal subjects, and measurement of LDL-C underestimates particle numbers and apoB100 levels. Other subjects have enlarged CE-enriched LDL particles and measurement of LDL-C overestimates the number of LDL particles and apoB100 molecules. Thus, LDL particle number or apoB100 levels are a more accurate predictor of cardiovascular risk in the setting of discordance between the percentiles for measures of cholesterol carried by LDL (LDL-C or Non-HDL-C) and particle number (apoB100 or LDL-P). Adapted from Sniderman, A.D. et al. Curr Opin Lipidol 2014, 25:461&#x02013;467.</p></div></div><p>Furthermore, all of the major atherogenic lipoproteins contain apoB (LDL, triglyceride rich remnants of VLDL, IDL, chylomicron remnants, and Lp(a)). LDL-C is routinely calculated using the Friedewald formula (LDL-C = TC &#x02013; HDL-C &#x02013; TG/5), but this formula is not accurate when serum TG levels are &#x0003e; 400 mg/dl. It has long been recognized that LDL-C underestimates risk of ASCVD in the setting of hypertriglyceridemia (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.467">467</a>). Non-HDL cholesterol is the mass of cholesterol in all of the apoB-containing particles: Non-HDL-C = TC &#x02013; HDL-C. The ATPIII guidelines recommended using Non-HDL-C to estimate risk of ASCVD, when TG &#x0003e; 200 mg/dL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.469">469</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.470">470</a>). A meta-analysis by Sniderman <i>et al.</i> found that Non-HDL-C was a slightly better marker of ASCVD risk than LDL-C, but apoB was far superior to Non-HDL-C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.471">471</a>). NMR spectroscopy is another way to measure LDL-P concentrations. <a href="/sites/books/NBK343489/table/lipid_athero.T.equivalent_percentiles_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroTequivalentpercentilesin">Table 2</a> includes selected percentiles for mean levels for the various LDL-related markers from the Framingham Offspring Study (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.472">472</a>). In an analysis of the Framingham Offspring Study, LDL-P determined by NMR was more strongly related to incident CVD events than LDL-C levels, and the ability of Non-HDL-C to predict risk was less than LDL-P, but better than LDL-C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.473">473</a>). In addition, they found that low LDL-P numbers were a better index of low CVD risk than low LDL-C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.473">473</a>). In contrast, an earlier meta-analysis from the Emerging Risk Factors Collaboration found LDL-C, Non-HDL and apoB to be equivalent markers of CVE (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.474">474</a>). The lack of difference may relate to the population studied. When the LDL particles have normal cholesterol content, then LDL-C, Non-HDL and apoB are equivalent markers (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F8/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF8" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF8">Figure 8</a>) of risk (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.471">471</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.473">473</a>). Interestingly, data from the Multi-Ethnic Study of Atherosclerosis (MESA) demonstrated that when LDL-C and LDL-P are discordant (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F8/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF8" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF8">Figure 8</a>), then LDL-P proves to be a better predictor of risk for incident CVD events than LDL-C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.475">475</a>).</p><div id="lipid_athero.T.equivalent_percentiles_in" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 2. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Equivalent Percentiles in the Framingham Offspring Study</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK343489/table/lipid_athero.T.equivalent_percentiles_in/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" headers="hd_h_lipid_athero.T.equivalent_percentiles_in_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">160</td><td headers="hd_h_lipid_athero.T.equivalent_percentiles_in_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">191</td><td headers="hd_h_lipid_athero.T.equivalent_percentiles_in_1_1_1_5" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">2210</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">Adapted from Contois JH, et al. <i>Clinical Chemistry</i> 2009; 55:407-419</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><p>For several decades the guidelines for treatment of hypercholesterolemia have focused on LDL-C levels both for risk stratification and as the principal target of therapy to prevent ASCVD. Indeed, therapeutic goals for LDL-C of &#x0003c; 100 mg/dL and &#x0003c; 70 mg/dL for subjects at high-risk and very high risk of CVE, respectively, were recommended by the 2004 update of the NCEP ATPIII, guidelines (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.469">469</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.470">470</a>). The 2013 ACC/AHA guidelines for treatment of hypercholesterolemia abandoned these targets in favor of recommending the use of high-intensity statins in high risk individuals (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.476">476</a>), there are numerous sets of guidelines that have maintained the recommendation for LDL-C targets, including those of the National Lipid Association (NLA) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.477">477</a>), the American Association for Clinical Endocrinologists (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.478">478</a>), and the European Guidelines (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.479">479</a>). The Canadian guidelines include targets for levels of apoB(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.480">480</a>), and the NLA guidelines include targets for both LDL-C and Non-HDL-C(477). <a href="/sites/books/NBK343489/table/lipid_athero.T.equivalent_percentiles_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroTequivalentpercentilesin">Table 2</a> includes the percentiles for mean levels for these various LDL markers from the Framingham Offspring Study. The levels of LDL-C shown in <a href="/sites/books/NBK343489/table/lipid_athero.T.equivalent_percentiles_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroTequivalentpercentilesin">Table 2</a> closely coincide with levels that have been widely used in guidelines for lipid management for decision-making regarding levels at which to initiate therapy and goals of therapy. The recent NLA guidelines recommend using both LDL-C and Non-HDL-C as targets of therapy with only two sets of targets: LDL-C &#x0003c; 70 mg/dL and Non-HDL-C &#x0003c; 100 mg/dL for very high-risk subjects and LDL-C &#x0003c; 100 mg/dL and Non-HDL-C &#x0003c; 130 mg/dL for high, moderate or low risk subjects (who qualify for drug therapy). Most recently, AHA/ACC published a new version of guidelines for cholesterol management (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.481">481</a>). These guidelines included evidence from recent 2 large randomized clinical end-point trials of PCSK9 inhibitors (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.454">454</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.455">455</a>) and a long-awaited ezetimibe trial in patients with recent acute coronary syndromes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.482">482</a>). The new guidelines re-introduced LDL-C treatment goals in some high-risk patient groups, such as those with high risk of ASCVD and those with very high baseline LDL-C. The new AHA/ACC guidelines also stated that elevated apoB particle number, elevated Lp(a) and hypertriglyceridemia are all additional risk factors for ASCVD.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Lp_a"><h3>Lp(a)</h3><p>Lp(a) has been shown to be an independent risk factor for atherothrombotic events, including heart attack, stroke and peripheral vascular disease, in multiple prospective studies (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.451">451</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.483">483</a>). The new AHA/ACC guidelines list elevated Lp(a) a one of the risk-enhancing factors for developing ASCVD (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.481">481</a>). Lp(a) consists of an LDL particle in which apoB100 is covalently linked via a disulfide bridge to apo(a), a glycoprotein with repeating Kringle units that share homology with plasminogen. Although apo(a) is synthesized by the liver, the Lp(a) particles are not formed in the liver but in the plasma. Despite being a modified LDL particle, Lp(a) levels are independent of LDL-C levels. The catabolism of Lp(a) is poorly understood, but Lp(a) is not cleared by the LDLR (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.484">484</a>). The number of repeating Kringle units is highly variable but largely genetically determined, and this contributes to tremendous heterogeneity in size of Lp(a). The plasma levels of Lp(a) vary tremendously in humans, and plasma Lp(a) levels are generally inversely related to the size of the apo(a) isoform (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.485">485</a>).
