HDL-P numbers can be quantified using nuclear magnetic resonance (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.754">754</a>) or calibrated ion mobility assays (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.755">755</a>). HDL-P was found to be inversely associated with carotid intima medial thickness (cIMT) and coronary heart disease (CHD) independent of LDL particle numbers and HDL-C levels in the large multi-ethnic study of atherosclerosis (MESA) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.756">756</a>). Importantly, HDL-P remains inversely associated to CHD after adjusting for triglycerides and apolipoprotein B (apoB), thus suggesting that HDL-P is far superior to HDL-C levels as a biomarker of ASCVD and events (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.757">757</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.758">758</a>). Furthermore, neither HDL-C levels nor HDL-P levels correlate to cholesterol efflux from macrophages; therefore, the rate of cholesterol efflux is still critical to understanding RCT and HDL function. Likewise, HDL quality is more important than apoA-I levels, which also do not correlate with HDL function, e.g. RCT (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.759">759</a>). Serum samples with identical apoA-I and HDL-C levels were found to have differing cholesterol acceptance capacities, mostly due to pre-beta HDL levels, which contributed to altered ABCA1-mediated cholesterol efflux (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.759">759</a>). These studies strongly suggest that HDL function (cholesterol efflux capacity), as opposed to HDL-C, HDL-P, and apoA-I levels, provide a more important risk assessment and better predictor of future events as well as a more reasoned therapeutic target for reducing CVD risk and events. However, clinical assays for apoA-I and HDL-P are widely available and well-established, whereas assays for cholesterol efflux capacity have not been standardized and remain a research tool at present.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.HDL_Composition_and_Analysi"><h3>HDL Composition and Analysis</h3><p>Historically, HDL have been isolated by density-gradient ultracentrifugation (DGUC) based on isopycnic equilibrium, and HDL have been defined by their density 1.063-1.21 g/mL since the 1950s (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.760">760</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.761">761</a>). Based on mass, HDL can also be separated from other lipoproteins by size-exclusion chromatography (fast protein liquid chromatography, FPLC), and HDL&#x02019;s molecular weight ranges from 175,000 - 360,000 Da (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.762">762</a>). In addition to DGUC and FPLC, affinity chromatography can also be used to purify HDL from plasma using antibodies against apoA-I (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.763">763</a>) or apoA-II, as HDL heterogeneity includes particles containing apoA-I:apoA-II (75%) or apoA-I only (25%) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.763">763</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.764">764</a>). Furthermore, asymmetric flow field-flow fractionation is now being used to isolate and characterize HDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.765">765</a>). HDL can also be separated by non-denaturing gradient gel electrophoresis, e.g. polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis. Large HDL (HDL<sub>2</sub>, 8.8-12.9 nm in diameter) and small HDL (HDL<sub>3</sub>, 7.2-8.8 nm) are both &#x003b1; migrating particles (high negative charge), whereas pre-&#x003b2; HDL (5.4-7 nm) are &#x003b2; migrating particles for which they are defined. To quantify pre-&#x003b2; HDL particles, 2-D gel electrophoresis is often used to separate pre-&#x003b2; from mature HDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.766">766</a>). HDL-P numbers can be quantified by either nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy or calibrated ion mobility assays. HDL can also be quantified and qualified by other methods, including vertical rotor ultracentrifugation, and transmission electron microscopy.</p><p>HDL are very dynamic and should be acknowledged as a heterogeneous pool of sub-classes with differing sizes, shapes, densities, protein compositions, and lipid diversity. Lipid-free apoA-I is secreted from the liver and small intestine as an amphipathic helix, and it quickly becomes lipidated by ABCA1 to form pre-&#x003b2; HDL, which then becomes discoidal after accepting phospholipid and free cholesterol from hepatocytes and peripheral cells. Upon further lipidation and cholesterol accumulation and esterification, nascent spherical HDL forms that range 7-12 nm in diameter. Mature HDL contains 3-4 apoA-I molecules of which 1 remains on the particle and the other apoA-I are free to (dis)associate (exchange) on and off the particle with other HDL. This is predominantly associated with rearrangement of HDL&#x02019;s aqueous phase and surface area (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.767">767</a>). As such, HDL are in a constant state of remodeling and interconversion. Each spherical HDL particle has approximately 50-130 phospholipids, 10-50 free cholesterol molecules, 30-90 CE molecules, and 10-20 triglyceride (TG) molecules (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.536">536</a>). Phosphatidylcholine makes up the largest amount of lipid on HDL (approximately 90%); however, over 200 species of lipids have been reported, including sphingolipids, acylglycerols, isoprenoids, glycerophospholipids, and vitamins (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.768">768</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.769">769</a>). The HDL proteome has been extensively studied and there is a general consensus of approximately 80 proteins (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.770">770</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.771">771</a>). In addition to apoA-I and apoA-II, HDL transports over a dozen other apolipoproteins, as well as many enzymes and other factors. HDL have also been found to transport small RNAs, namely microRNAs (miRNA), which were found to be altered in hypercholesterolemia and atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.772">772</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.773">773</a>). Most interestingly, HDL have been demonstrated to transport a wide-variety of exogenous non-host small RNAs, including rRNA and tRNA fragments derived from bacterial and fungal species present in the microbiome and environment (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.774">774</a>).The size of HDL is determined by the amount of CE and triglyceride (TG) in the hydrophobic core, and HDL is generally separated into 5 sub-classes based on size. Distinct HDL sub-species have been associated with CVD risk, and the sub-species have differential biological functions, e.g. large HDL are less anti-inflammatory (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.775">775</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.777">777</a>). Many of the cargo or components of HDL are enriched in the small HDL sub-class which provides many of the alternative functions to the total HDL pool (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.778">778</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.779">779</a>). The concentration of all HDL particles in plasma is approximately 20 umol/L; however, small HDL particles are the most abundant sub-class at approximately 10 umol/L. HDL are heterogeneous particles that transport a wide-variety of proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids, which confer many of HDL&#x02019;s biological properties and beneficial functions in health and dysfunction in specific diseases.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.HDL_Cell_Signaling"><h3>HDL Cell Signaling</h3><p>Many of HDL&#x02019;s cellular functions &#x02013; cell survival, proliferation, vasodilation -- are mediated by HDL-induced cell signaling cascades (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.780">780</a>). As such, HDL can be characterized as hormone-like agonists. Although substantial work still remains in identifying HDL binding proteins and receptors on the cell surface, HDL have been found to activate many signaling cascades through various receptors. The most studied example of this is HDL&#x02019;s ability to bind to the plasma membrane and through cell signaling mobilize cholesterol from intracellular stores in organelles to the plasma membrane for efflux. This has been attributed to HDL-induced activation of protein kinase C (PKC) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.781">781</a>). Specifically, apoA-I binds to ABCA1 and activates phosphatidylcholine lipases, which activate PKC leading to the movement of cellular cholesterol from intracellular stores to the plasma membrane for efflux, as well as PKC-mediated phosphorylation of ABCA1, which increases the transporter&#x02019;s stability and efflux activity (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.782">782</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.784">784</a>). This is a prime example of HDL-induced cell signaling that contributes to HDL cholesterol efflux capacity, which reduces the cholesterol burden for macrophages in the lesion, prevents foam cell formation, and antagonizes atherogenesis. Other HDL-induced signaling pathways that result in increased cholesterol and lipid efflux include protein kinase A (PKA) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.785">785</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.786">786</a>), cell division control protein 42 (Cdc42) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.787">787</a>), and Janus kinases-2 (JAK2) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.788">788</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.789">789</a>) cascades. HDL (i.e. apoA-I)-induced cell signaling through ABCA1 also suppresses macrophage M1 phenotype activation and pro-inflammatory cytokine production (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>), and promotes M2 phenotype anti-inflammatory cytokine secretion (e.g. interleukin 10 (IL-10)) through JAK2 signaling and activation of signal transducer and activator of transcription 3 (STAT3) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.75">75</a>). In addition, the apoA-I:ABCA1:JAK2 axis was reported to suppress inflammation in endothelial cells through cyclooxygenase-2 (COX-2) activation leading to increased prostaglandins (PGI<sub>2</sub>), which also suppresses atherogenesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.790">790</a>). HDL have also been reported to induce cell signaling through SR-BI. HDL binding to SR-BI&#x02019;s extracellular loop was reported to trigger activation of SR-BI&#x02019;s cytoplasmic C terminal domain leading to the phosphorylation of protein kinase Src and activation of both liver kinase B1 (LKB1) and calmodulin-dependent protein kinase (CAMK) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.791">791</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.792">792</a>). This results in cell signaling through downstream kinases &#x02013; AMP-activated protein kinases (AMPK) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.792">792</a>), protein kinase Akt (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.791">791</a>), and mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK)(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.791">791</a>) &#x02013; which ultimately regulates angiogenesis (ubiquitin ligase Siah (Siah1/2) and hypoxia-inducible factor 1&#x003b1; (HIF1&#x003b1;) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.793">793</a>)), insulin sensitivity (glucose transporter 4 (Glut4)(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.794">794</a>)), re-vascularization (Rac1(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.795">795</a>)), and vasodilation (COX(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.796">796</a>), endothelial nitric oxide synthase (eNOS)(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.797">797</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.798">798</a>)). Interestingly, macrophage SR-BI has recently been shown to mediate efferocytosis (phagocytosis of dead cells) in the setting of atherosclerosis via a Src/Akt/Rac1 signaling pathway, reducing necrosis in lesions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.185">185</a>). All of these downstream effects contribute to HDL function, and to a lesser degree atherogenesis.</p><p>The most robust HDL signaling activation is mediated by bioactive lipids on HDL, namely the lysosphingolipid sphingosine-1-phosphate (S1P). A majority of S1P in circulation is associated with HDL, and HDL-S1P activates the G-coupled S1P receptors (S1P<sub>1-5</sub>) on the surface of many vascular cell types, including macrophages, endothelial cells, and smooth muscle cells. Activation of S1P<sub>1</sub> and S1P<sub>2</sub> receptors turns on a host of signaling cascades and factors that directly contribute to the many anti-atherogenic properties of HDL, including increasing endothelial barrier function (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.799">799</a>) and angiogenesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.800">800</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.801">801</a>) while decreasing inflammation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.802">802</a>) and apoptosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.803">803</a>). HDL were also found to inhibit smooth muscle migration through S1P signaling, a key factor in restenosis and plaque development (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.804">804</a>). All of these are critical processes to atherogenesis. In support of these studies, subjects with CAD were found to have decreased HDL-S1P levels (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.805">805</a>). The key terminal effector factors in these G-protein receptor signaling cascades are focal adhesion kinase (FAK), nuclear factor &#x003ba; beta (NF-&#x003ba;B), nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH) oxidase, eNOS, STAT3, and B-cell lymphoma-extra-large (Bcl-xl) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.780">780</a>). This HDL-S1P signaling pathway has also been linked to vasorelaxation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.806">806</a>) and cytoprotection (e.g. cardiomyocytes) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.807">807</a>). In addition to these direct pathways, HDL also likely activates cell signaling indirectly through ATP (&#x003b2;-ATPase/P2Y<sub>12/13</sub>)(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.808">808</a>) or toll-like receptors (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.809">809</a>). Collectively, HDL induced cell signaling in vascular and inflammatory cells underlies HDL&#x02019;s anti-atherogenic properties in health, and deficits in HDL signaling likely link HDL dysfunction in metabolic diseases to increased risk of atherosclerosis.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.AntiInflammatory_HDL"><h3>Anti-Inflammatory HDL</h3><p>Outside reverse cholesterol transport, HDL&#x02019;s anti-inflammatory properties have been the most extensively studied HDL function and likely play a large role in HDL&#x02019;s anti-atherogenicity (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>). HDL&#x02019;s anti-inflammatory properties are conferred by numerous mechanisms in many types of cells. In addition to providing the vascular barrier, endothelial cells control vascular inflammation through expressing adhesion molecules that aid in monocyte adhesion and ultimate migration into the atherosclerotic lesion. Moreover, activated endothelial cells secrete cytokines and recruit monocytes through chemokine release. The induction of adhesion molecules, cytokines, and chemokines in activated endothelial cells is largely due to NF-&#x003ba;B transcriptional activation. In humans, injection of apoA-I resulted in decreased adhesion molecule expression in atherosclerotic plaques (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.810">810</a>). One mechanism by which HDL suppresses endothelial cell and monocyte activation is through inhibiting NF-kB activity by attenuating IkB kinase activity (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.811">811</a>). Nonetheless, HDL decreases adhesion molecule expression through multiple mechanisms. Cells pre-treated with HDL or apoA-I are protected from TNF&#x003b1; or oxidized LDL (oxLDL)-induced adhesion molecule expression. In addition, HDL binding to SR-BI may also contribute to inhibition of adhesion molecule expression, as SR-BI-mediated Akt activation promoted heme oxygenase-1 expression. In addition, up-regulation of 3-beta-hydroxysteroid-delta 24 (DHCR24) by HDL binding to SR-BI was reported to underlie HDL&#x02019;s ability to suppress adhesion molecules (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.812">812</a>). Furthermore, HDL suppression of intracellular adhesion molecule-1 (ICAM-1) in endothelial cells was found to be mediated, in part, through the transfer of miR-223 to recipient cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.773">773</a>). Recent studies also suggest that TGF&#x003b2; and AMPK also contribute to HDL&#x02019;s suppression of adhesion molecule expression (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.813">813</a>).</p><p>In addition to HDL&#x02019;s profound effects on vascular endothelium, HDL suppresses myelopoiesis, monocyte recruitment, macrophage activation, proliferation, and emigration from atherosclerotic lesions. Similar to its impact on endothelial cells, HDL also suppresses adhesion molecule expression in monocytes, which inhibits monocyte adhesion and migration to atherosclerotic lesions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.814">814</a>). HDL and apoA-I were demonstrated to suppress CD11b expression on human monocytes through both ABCA1-dependent and independent mechanisms (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.814">814</a>). HDL inhibition of monocyte activation, which includes suppression of cytokines and adhesion molecules, is mediated through both peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor gamma (PPAR&#x003b3;) and NF-kB transcription factors (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.815">815</a>). Suppression of chemokine and cytokines in myeloid cells inhibits infiltration and migration of circulating monocytes, and thus antagonizes atherosclerosis. HDL have also been reported to mediate macrophage reprogramming through the transcription factor ATF3 that reduces Toll-like receptor signaling (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.816">816</a>). Importantly, much of HDL&#x02019;s (and apoA-I&#x02019;s) inhibition of macrophage activation is mediated through altering cholesterol levels in plasma membrane lipid rafts through cholesterol efflux mediated by ABCG1/SR-BI and ABCA1; however, apoA-I induced signaling through ABCA1 and the JAK/STAT pathway independent of cholesterol efflux may also contribute to HDL&#x02019;s effect, as described above (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.75">75</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.817">817</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.814">814</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.818">818</a>). HDL have also been demonstrated to promote macrophage emigration through removing excess cholesterol and induction of signaling pathways (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.208">208</a>). In addition to HDL&#x02019;s impact on monocytes and macrophages, HDL also strongly suppresses neutrophil activation and vascular smooth muscle cell secretion of monocyte chemoattractant protein-1 (MCP1) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.819">819</a>).</p><p>In addition to HDL&#x02019;s roles in innate immunity, recent evidence suggests that HDL play multiple roles in adaptive immunity (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.820">820</a>). Mice lacking apoA-I develop autoimmunity when challenged with a high cholesterol (diet and background, <i>Ldlr<sup>-/-</sup></i>), which includes T cell activation and production of autoantibodies (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.821">821</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.822">822</a>). This phenotype was rescued by apoA-I injections. HDL have also been reported to repress both antigen-presenting cell (APC) activation of T cells and T cell activation of monocytes, thus preventing the secretion of proinflammatory cytokines and chemokines (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.823">823</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.824">824</a>). ApoA-I also prevents the phenotypic switching of T-regs into pro-inflammatory follicular helper T cells during atheroprogression (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.92">92</a>). Moreover, cholesterol efflux to HDL and apoA-I have been reported to suppress myelopoiesis and proliferation of myelopoietic stem and progenitor cells, as loss of function for both <i>Abca1</i> and <i>Abcg1</i> in mice resulted in increased myelopoiesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.820">820</a>). Injection of apoA-I was also found to rescue this phenotype (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.825">825</a>). In addition, HDL and cholesterol efflux were reported to suppress megakaryocyte progenitor proliferation, platelet levels, and thrombocytosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.826">826</a>). Collectively, HDL and apoA-I inhibit circulating levels of hematopoietic progenitor cells, monocytes, neutrophils, and platelets all of which contribute to HDL&#x02019;s capacity to limit inflammation and atherosclerosis.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.Antithrombotic_HDL"><h3>Antithrombotic HDL</h3><p>Another anti-atherogenic function of HDL is the capacity to directly and indirectly inhibit platelet activation, aggregation, and thrombus formation (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>). HDL-C levels were found to be inversely associated with thrombus formation in humans (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.827">827</a>). HDL is required to remove excess cholesterol from the plasma membrane of platelets for proper function, and platelets isolated from mice lacking SR-BI to mediate cholesterol efflux to HDL were found to be more susceptible to activation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.828">828</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.829">829</a>). Both HDL and cyclodextrin-mediated cholesterol efflux were found to inhibit platelet aggregation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.828">828</a>). However, HDL-induced cell signaling through binding to glycoprotein IIb/IIIa on the surface of platelets was reported to activate phospholipase C (PLC) and PKC, thus leading to flux through the Na+/H+ antiport system (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.717">717</a>). This pathway can result in alkalization of the cytoplasm and calcium release, which can reduce platelet activation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.830">830</a>). Furthermore, HDL dose-dependently inhibits stimulated platelet activation, which leads to reduced platelet aggregation, granule secretion and fibrinogen binding. In rats, apoA-I injections inhibited thrombus formation and reduced thrombus mass (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.831">831</a>). HDL&#x02019;s anti-thrombotic effects are also mediated, in part, through HDL&#x02019;s ability to inhibit tissue factor and factors X, Va, and VIIIa (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.832">832</a>). HDL also prevents thrombus formation through cell signaling and nitric oxide (NO) production in endothelial cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.828">828</a>), and suppression of tissue factor and platelet-activating factor expression in endothelial cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.833">833</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.834">834</a>). HDL also reduces erythrocyte influence on thrombus formation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.835">835</a>). Collectively, HDL has multiple biological mechanisms that inhibit thrombus formation, and thus, contribute to HDL&#x02019;s anti-atherogenic properties.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.ProVasodilatory_HDL"><h3>Pro-Vasodilatory HDL</h3><p>The endothelium significantly contributes to vascular tone, and HDL confer protection against endothelial cell activation, apoptosis, and loss of barrier function, which is critical to atherogenesis. HDL have been reported to induce endothelium-dependent vasodilation in aortic rings (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.806">806</a>), and individuals with low HDL have reduced endothelium-dependent vasorelaxation (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.741">741</a>). HDL&#x02019;s benefit to endothelial cells is largely mediated by cell signaling through phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K) and Akt and is induced by bioactive lipids and associated proteins on HDL, including lysosulfatide, S1P, and sphingosylphosphorylcholine (SPC) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.791">791</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.798">798</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.806">806</a>). A key outcome of HDL-induced cell signaling is the production of NO (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>) through both signaling induced phosphorylation of eNOS and increased eNOS expression (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.791">791</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.836">836</a>). HDL can trigger eNOS-phosphorylation through SR-BI, S1P receptor (S1P<sub>1-5</sub>), and ABCG1-mediated cholesterol efflux (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.806">806</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.837">837</a>). HDL-induced NO underlies many of HDL&#x02019;s beneficial properties to endothelial cells, including HDL-induced vasodilation, tightening of cell-to-cell junctions and increased barrier function, differentiation of endothelial progenitor cells, cell survival and proliferation, cell migration, inhibition of apoptosis, and suppression of adhesion molecule expression. In addition, HDL also has NO-independent properties on endothelial cells, including induced proliferation, increased barrier function, suppressed inflammation and decreased apoptosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.838">838</a>). These studies clearly define a beneficial role for HDL in vascular integrity, which underlies HDL protection against atherosclerosis.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.AntiApoptotic_HDL"><h3>Anti-Apoptotic HDL</h3><p>HDL have multiple anti-apoptotic properties that enhance cell survival (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>). By various metrics, HDL support mitochondrial function and prevent the release of apoptotic signals, including cytochrome C (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.205">205</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.839">839</a>). Moreover, HDL drives the expression of Bcl-xl, which is a strong anti-apoptotic factor and suppresses Bid, which is a pro-apoptic protein (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.839">839</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.840">840</a>). HDL mediates these gene expression changes through cell signaling and NO production through activation of surface receptors by HDL-associated proteins and bioactive lipids, including apolipoprotein J (apoJ) and S1P (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.803">803</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.840">840</a>). In addition, there are likely alternative anti-apoptotic mechanisms resulting from HDL-induced signaling. Nonetheless, HDL has been demonstrated to suppress apoptosis in endothelial cells (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>) activated with tumor necrosis factor (TNF&#x003b1;) and oxLDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.839">839</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.841">841</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.842">842</a>). HDL proteins (apolipoprotein M, apoM) and apoM-binding lipids (S1P) contribute to HDL&#x02019;s ability to increase tight junctions and endothelial cell survival (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.843">843</a>). Mice deficient in apoM have reduced S1P levels and loss of endothelium barrier function (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.843">843</a>). HDL&#x02019;s ability to support the endothelium barrier function is a key feature of its anti-atherosclerosis properties and represents a classic example of HDL&#x02019;s control of cellular gene expression and phenotype that are beneficial to vascular health. However, HDL also have many capacities in the extracellular space (e.g. plasma) that protect against atherosclerosis.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.AntiOxidative_HDL"><h3>Anti-Oxidative HDL</h3><p>A key factor in monocyte activation and chemotaxis in the vascular wall is the accumulation of oxLDL, which is more pro-inflammatory and pro-atherogenic than unmodified LDL. LDL can become oxidized by a variety of endogenous mechanisms (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.844">844</a>). In the vascular wall, LDL can be modified (oxidized) by many cell types, including vascular smooth muscle cells, endothelial cells, and macrophages (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.776">776</a>). Remarkably, HDL prevents the oxidation of LDL (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>) and recent evidence suggests that this may occur through 4 distinct proteins circulating on HDL &#x02013; apoA-I (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.845">845</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.846">846</a>), LCAT (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.847">847</a>), lipoprotein-associated phospholipase A2 (Lp-PLA2)(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.848">848</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.849">849</a>), and paraoxonase 1 (PON1) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.430">430</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.846">846</a>). First, HDL can simply soak up oxidized lipids or oxidizing factors from cells preventing their association with LDL and their modification of LDL lipids and proteins. In addition, HDL removes lipid hydroperoxides from LDL particles (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.846">846</a>). Specifically, small apoAI containing HDL particles are the most efficient at accepting lipid hydroperoxides, which are reduced to their inactive lipid hydroxides via oxidation of the methionine residues in apoA-I (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.850">850</a>). Compared to apoA-II the methionine residues in apoA-I are more conformationally conducive to reducing lipid hydroperoxides (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.851">851</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.852">852</a>). In addition, HDL with low surface free cholesterol and sphingomyelin are more efficient at accepting lipid hydroperoxides (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.745">745</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.853">853</a>). The capacity of HDL to prevent oxidation via this mechanism is also maintained by the selective removal of HDL lipid hydroperoxides and hydroxides by hepatocyte SR-BI (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.854">854</a>). In addition, ApoA-I methionine sulfoxide is reduced to methionine by methionine sulfoxide reductases.(<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.850">850</a>). LCAT circulates on HDL and has also been reported to block LDL oxidation, as LCAT over-expression in mice reduced LDL oxidation as determined by reduced LDL autoantibodies (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.855">855</a>). Lp-PLA<sub>2</sub> appears to be pro-atherogenic on LDL and anti-atherogenic on HDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.856">856</a>). Its activity on HDL likely contributes to HDL&#x02019;s anti-oxidative capacity, as inhibition of HDL-associated Lp-PLA<sub>2</sub> attenuated HDL&#x02019;s ability to block LDL oxidation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.848">848</a>). The strongest anti-oxidative HDL protein is likely PON1. Over-expression of PON1 in mice confers enhanced HDL anti-oxidative capacity, and PON1 itself prevents LDL oxidation in vitro (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.432">432</a>). Most importantly, HDL isolated from mice lacking PON1 have reduced ability to prevent LDL oxidation. HDL&#x02019;s anti-oxidative capacity likely plays a large role in preventing inflammation and atherogenesis, and like many of the other alternative functions, confer HDL&#x02019;s beneficial role in health.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.HDL_Intercellular_Communica"><h3>HDL Intercellular Communication</h3><p>HDL also likely participate in intercellular communication through the transfer of nucleic acids between tissues. Recently, HDL have been reported to transport miRNA (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>), which are small non-coding RNAs that suppress gene expression through binding to complimentary target sites in the 3&#x02019; untranslated region of mRNAs, and thus inhibit translation and induce mRNA degradation (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.772">772</a>). Most interestingly, the HDL-miRNA profile is significantly altered in hypercholesterolemia and atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.772">772</a>). miRNAs have been reported to be exported from macrophages to HDL, and HDL has been demonstrated to transfer specific miRNAs to recipient hepatoma cells (Huh7) and endothelial cells, likely through HDL&#x02019;s receptor SR-BI (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.773">773</a>). In endothelial cells, HDL was found to deliver miR-223 to recipient cells, where it directly targeted intracellular adhesion molecule-1 (ICAM-1) expression (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK343489/figure/lipid_athero.F10/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="figlipidatheroF10" rid-ob="figoblipidatheroF10">Figure 10</a>), and thus inhibited neutrophil adhesion to the cells (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.773">773</a>). miR-223 is not transcribed or processed in endothelial cells and HDL delivery of mature miR-223 to endothelium likely confers, in part, HDL&#x02019;s anti-inflammatory capacity associated with adhesion molecule suppression. Future studies are needed to determine the physiological relevance and functional impact of HDL-miRNAs in humans and animal models in the context of atherosclerosis and other inflammatory diseases.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.AntiInfectious_HDL"><h3>Anti-Infectious HDL</h3><p>HDL also contributes to innate immunity by modulating immune cell function. However, this hypothesis has not been extensively studied in the context of atherosclerosis. HDL are anti-infectious, anti-parasitic, and anti-viral. HDL have the unique capacity to prevent endotoxic shock and readily binds to lipopolysaccharides (LPS) and contributes to removing LPS through biliary excretion thus aiding innate immunity (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.857">857</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.859">859</a>). Amongst the many proteins that circulate on HDL, apolipoprotein L1 (apo-L1) (also known as trypanosome lytic factor) is present in specific sub-classes of HDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.860">860</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.861">861</a>). This factor kills <i>Trypanosome brucei</i> and <i>Trypanosome brucei rhdesiense</i>, parasites that cause sleeping sickness, through creating ionic pores in endosomes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.860">860</a>-<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.862">862</a>). Although promising, future studies are required to define how HDL regulation of innate immunity contributes to the inhibition of atherogenesis.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.HDL_Dysfunction"><h3>HDL Dysfunction</h3><p>HDL confer many anti-atherogenic properties that are lost in atherosclerosis and other inflammatory and metabolic diseases. These include 9 key processes &#x02013;</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Loss of cholesterol efflux capacity from macrophages</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Reduced ability to inhibit LDL oxidation</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Decreased vasodilation through reduced NO production in endothelial cells</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Reduced ability to inhibit monocyte chemotactic activity</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Loss of the ability to metabolize hydroperoxides on erythrocyte membranes</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Reduced ability to suppress TNF&#x003b1;-induced NF-&#x003ba;B activation and adhesion molecule expression</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Loss of anti-apoptotic capacity in endothelial cells</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Decreased capacity to block TNF&#x003b1;-induced NADPH oxidase activity and superoxide production</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Suppression of cytokine inhibition in activated inflammatory cells.</div></li></ul><p>Many of these defects are due to changes in HDL cargo, e.g. decreased PON1 levels or increased serum amyloid A (SAA) levels. Moreover, changes in the content of bioactive lipids or increased oxidative modifications to HDL&#x02019;s lipids and protein cargo likely confer dysfunction. HDL-miRNAs have been shown to be significantly altered in hypercholesterolemia and atherosclerosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.772">772</a>). It is unknown how these changes contribute to HDL&#x02019;s loss of anti-atherogenic properties, but they hold great potential for future studies. In CHD, acute coronary syndrome (ACS), and ischemic cardiomyopathy, HDL have reduced ability to inhibit oxidation of LDL, likely through reduced PON1 levels as reported in CHD (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.845">845</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.863">863</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.864">864</a>). Loss of PON1 also reduces HDL&#x02019;s ability to prevent oxidation of its own lipids and proteins, which has been reported in metabolic syndrome as oxidation of apoA-I impairs HDL&#x02019;s RCT and anti-inflammatory functions (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.865">865</a>). Reduced HDL-PON1 levels are also found in other cardiometabolic diseases, including type 2 diabetic mellitus (T2DM) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.866">866</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.867">867</a>), type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1DM) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.868">868</a>), rheumatoid arthritis (RA) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.869">869</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.870">870</a>), dyslipidemia (e.g. hyperalphalipoproteinemia (HALP) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.871">871</a>)), and patients after cardiac surgery (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.872">872</a>). In subjects with ACS and CAD, HDL have been reported to have decreased ability to prevent endothelial cell apoptosis likely through decreased activation (phosphorylation) of Bcl-xl and increased activation of Bcl-2, which are anti-apoptotic and pro-apoptotic proteins, respectively (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.840">840</a>). Loss of HDL&#x02019;s anti-apoptotic capacity has been proposed to be due to increased apoCIII and possibly decreased clusterin levels on HDL (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.840">840</a>). HDL from subjects with CHD also have decreased ability to prevent monocyte adhesion to endothelial cells and recruitment in arterial wall co-cultures, which could be associated with reduced PON1 levels amongst other cargo (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.845">845</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.873">873</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.874">874</a>). HDL from ischemic cardiomyopathy and CAD subjects also have reduced cholesterol efflux acceptance capacity, which likely leads to increased foam cell formation in the atherosclerotic lesion and increased atherogenesis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.746">746</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.863">863</a>). Although the molecular basis for all of HDL&#x02019;s loss of anti-atherogenicity in CHD is not known, other functions of HDL are compromised in these subjects, including the ability to reduce hydroperoxides on erythrocyte membranes (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.875">875</a>). This loss of HDL&#x02019;s anti-oxidant capacity is also found in T2DM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.875">875</a>) and T1DM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.868">868</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.876">876</a>). HDL in metabolic syndrome have been reported to have decreased capacity to prevent oxidation of LDL and inhibit endothelial cell apoptosis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.877">877</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.878">878</a>). This loss of anti-atherogenic properties is also found in hypertension (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.879">879</a>), T2DM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.867">867</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.880">880</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.881">881</a>), end-stage renal disease (ESRD) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.882">882</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.883">883</a>), RA (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.869">869</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.870">870</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.884">884</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.885">885</a>), systemic erythematosus lupus (SLE) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.884">884</a>), obstructive sleep apnea (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.886">886</a>), and dyslipidemia (HALP) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.871">871</a>). Reduced ability of HDL to stimulate NO production from endothelial cells and decreased vasorelaxation properties are reported for T2DM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.887">887</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.888">888</a>), T1DM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.889">889</a>), mild chronic kidney disease (CKD) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.890">890</a>), and rare forms of autoimmunity (ALPS) (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.891">891</a>). Loss of HDL-mediated cholesterol efflux capacity has been found in patients with hyperhomocysteinemia (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.892">892</a>), sepsis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.893">893</a>), psoriasis (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.894">894</a>), SLE (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.895">895</a>), RA (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.885">885</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.896">896</a>), ESRD (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.897">897</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.898">898</a>), and T2DM (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.899">899</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.900">900</a>). Not only does HDL dysfunction result from loss of key proteins and cargo, HDL can gain pro-atherogenic cargo and properties in cardiometabolic diseases. Due to loss of PON1, HDL accumulate malonaldehydes, which inhibits NO production through increased phosphorylation of eNOS through LOX-1 receptor signaling (<a class="bk_pop" href="#lipid_athero.REF.901">901</a>).</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.HDL_Summary"><h3>HDL Summary</h3><p>Years of sound epidemiological studies have clearly established an inverse relationship between HDL-C levels and risk of CVD. Nevertheless, recent GWAS studies suggest that individuals with high HDL-C levels are not protected from CVD. Furthermore, clinical studies aimed at raising HDL-C levels through niacin and CETP inhibitors have failed to reduce risk of cardiovascular events and have been stopped prematurely due to lack of efficacy or increased number of events. Although they&#x02019;re often lumped together, HDL-C levels do not represent HDL particle numbers or HDL function (e.g. cholesterol efflux capacity); both of which have been reported to be better indicators of CVD risk than HDL-C. In addition to HDL&#x02019;s transport of cholesterol and lipids in the RCT pathway, HDL transports a wide-variety of cargo, including a diverse group of proteins, small RNAs, bioactive lipids, and many other small molecules. These alternative cargos may confer many of HDL&#x02019;s alternative functions outside of RCT. In fact, HDL have many beneficial properties, including anti-inflammatory, anti-oxidative, anti-thrombotic, anti-infectious, anti-apoptotic, intercellular communication, and pro-vasodilatory capacities. Recently, HDL dysfunction has been reported in many cardiometabolic diseases, including CAD, T2D, and CKD. Current and future challenges include the need to better define HDL anti-atherogenic properties in health and pro-atherogenic influences in disease to better control HDL function to potentially prevent and treat CVD.</p></div></div><div id="lipid_athero.ACKNOWLEGEMENTS"><h2 id="_lipid_athero_ACKNOWLEGEMENTS_">ACKNOWLEGEMENTS</h2><p>This work was supported in part by National Institutes of Health grants HL116263 and <a href="/nuccore/1051905757" class="bk_tag" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=nuccore">HL127173</a>.</p></div><div id="lipid_athero.REFERENCES"><h2 id="_lipid_athero_REFERENCES_">REFERENCES</h2><dl class="temp-labeled-list"><dt>1.</dt><dd><div class="bk_ref" id="lipid_athero.REF.1">Mozaffarian D, Benjamin EJ, Go AS, Arnett DK, Blaha MJ, Cushman M, de Ferranti S, Despres JP, Fullerton HJ, Howard VJ, Huffman MD, Judd SE, Kissela BM, Lackland DT, Lichtman JH, Lisabeth LD, Liu S, Mackey RH, Matchar DB, McGuire DK, Mohler ER 3rd, Moy CS, Muntner P, Mussolino ME, Nasir K, Neumar RW, Nichol G, Palaniappan L, Pandey DK, Reeves MJ, Rodriguez CJ, Sorlie PD, Stein J, Towfighi A, Turan TN, Virani SS, Willey JZ, Woo D, Yeh RW, Turner MB.
